x = 3; if (x > 2) { cube(1); } else if (x > 0) sphere(1); else cylinder(1);
Implementer: Guarantee the untaken branch is not evaluated (observable via echo/warnings). Reproduce branch scoping exactly; conditional values require the ternary, and scripts rely on inner assignment NOT leaking.

intersection_for (statement)

syntax pre-2015.03 
intersection_for (var1 = iterable1 [, var2 = iterable2, ...]) statement-or-{block}

Identical header and iteration semantics to for, but the per-iteration results are INTERSECTED instead of unioned.

varN = iterableNSame forms as for: vector, range, string, scalar; multiple bindings = cross product, first binding outermost.
bodyChild statement or block; multiple statements within ONE iteration are still implicitly unioned with each other, then that per-iteration union participates in the overall intersection.

Evaluates the body once per iteration exactly like for, then combines the N per-iteration results with n-ary intersection. Exists because wrapping a plain for in intersection() is useless (the for's implicit union arrives as a single child). All iteration results must share dimensionality (mixing 2D and 3D is the standard boolean error).

Edge cases & oddities
  • Zero iterations (empty vector): empty result — VERIFY: whether identical to intersection() of nothing (empty geometry, no warning).
  • Exactly one iteration: result is that iteration's geometry unchanged (single-child intersection passes through).
  • Multiple statements inside one iteration's block are unioned first; only across iterations does intersection apply.
  • All range/scalar/string iterable quirks and warnings are shared verbatim with for — same deprecated reversed-range swap, same step-0 warning.
  • Mixing 2D and 3D results across iterations is an error: 'ERROR: Mixing 2D and 3D objects is not supported' — VERIFY exact text/position.
  • Disjoint per-iteration geometry yields empty result silently.
  • There is NO comprehension analogue — intersection_for is statement-level only.
  • intersection_for is a plain identifier-like keyword builtin, not reserved: VERIFY whether a user module named intersection_for can shadow it.
intersection_for (a = [0, 60, 120]) rotate([0, 0, a]) cube([12, 4, 4], center = true);
Implementer: Share the entire iteration engine with for; only the combining node differs (union vs intersection). Probe empty-iteration and single-iteration cases against the oracle's .csg export.

let (expression)

syntax 2015.03 
let (name1 = expr1 [, name2 = expr2, ...]) expression

Expression-level local bindings: evaluates bindings sequentially (each sees the previous), then evaluates the trailing expression in that extended scope; the whole thing is an expression.

nameN = exprNSequential bindings; exprN may reference name1..name(N-1) and all outer-scope names. $-prefixed names allowed and become dynamically scoped for calls inside the body expression.
expressionThe result expression, evaluated with all bindings in scope.

Creates a new lexical scope shadowing outer names. Bindings are evaluated eagerly, strictly left to right, each binding visible to later ones — unlike top-level assignment there is NO last-write-wins reordering across let bindings; textual order is evaluation order. Usable anywhere an expression is allowed, including inside function bodies, default parameter values, and comprehension clause chains (see the comprehension let entry for clause form).

Edge cases & oddities
  • Repeating a name within one let (let(a=1, a=a+1) ...): the second binding sees the first and shadows it — VERIFY: whether a reassignment warning is emitted and whether the result is 2 (sequential) in 2021.01.
  • let(a=a) x binds a to the OUTER a (RHS evaluated before the new binding exists) — VERIFY: or undef-with-warning if no outer a.
  • Empty binding list let() expr: VERIFY whether it parses (believed legal, no-op).
  • Binding a $-variable (let($fn=64) f()) affects $-lookup dynamically in all calls made while evaluating the body expression.
  • let binds tighter than ?: — 'cond ? let(a=1) a : b' works; precedence/extent of the body expression is 'as far right as possible' — VERIFY grammar extent (let(...) a + b covers a+b).
  • Bindings are evaluated even if the body never uses them (eager, so their warnings/side echoes fire) — VERIFY eagerness with echo-expression inside a binding.
  • let does not create geometry scope — it is pure expression machinery; the statement form is a separate construct (2019.05).
echo(let (a = 2, b = a * 3) a + b); // ECHO: 8
Implementer: Implement as nested environment frames; sequential visibility is load-bearing (differs from a scope's last-write-wins assignments). Pin duplicate-name-in-one-let behavior and grammar extent of the body against the oracle.

let (statement)

syntax 2019.05 
let (name1 = expr1 [, ...]) statement-or-{block}

Statement/module form of let (2019.05+): binds names sequentially in a new child scope over its child statement(s); the modern replacement for deprecated assign().

nameN = exprNSame sequential-binding rules as let-expression; $-variables allowed and dynamically scope over the subtree.
bodySingle child statement or { } block; children are implicitly unioned; the bindings are visible throughout.

Evaluates bindings left to right, each seeing earlier ones, then instantiates the child(ren) in that scope. The whole let statement is one child of its parent (implicit union of its own children). Unlike a { } block with plain assignments, let makes the sequential-binding intent explicit and, like all statement scopes, cannot mutate outer variables.

Edge cases & oddities
  • 2019.05+ only: on 2015.03 this is a syntax error — feature-test with version_num()>=20190500.
  • Bindings are sequential (let(a=1,b=a+1) sees a) — identical to expression form; VERIFY duplicate-name behavior matches expression form.
  • Plain assignments inside the child block coexist with let bindings; those block assignments follow normal last-write-wins within the block scope.
  • let-statement bindings shadow outer names for the subtree only; nothing leaks out.
  • $-bindings (let($fs=0.5){...}) affect all descendant instantiations and function calls dynamically.
  • Modifier characters may prefix the let statement and apply to its whole result.
  • verify vs oracle let() with empty binding list as a statement parses.
  • Not to be confused with the comprehension let clause (expression context) — three syntactic homes total for let.
let (s = 4, h = s * 2) { cube([s, s, h]); }
Implementer: Reuse the let-expression binding engine; wrap children in an implicit-union group node. Emit no deprecation (contrast assign).

List comprehensions

each

syntax 2019.05 
each expression

Splice operator (2019.05+): inside a vector literal or comprehension chain, contributes the ELEMENTS of a vector or range (flattening one level) instead of the value itself; a non-iterable value contributes itself.

expressionValue to splice: a vector contributes its elements in order; a range is expanded and contributes each numeric step; any other value (number, boolean, undef, function) contributes itself as a single element.

each is the one-level flatten/append primitive: [each [1,2], each [3:5]] == [1,2,3,4,5]. It is legal directly inside vector literals mixed with plain elements, and as the terminal producer of a comprehension chain ([for (r=rows) each r] flattens a matrix one level). each of a range is the idiomatic way to materialize a range into a vector.

Edge cases & oddities
  • each on a nested vector flattens exactly ONE level: [each [[1],[2]]] == [[1],[2]] spliced to [[1],[2]] — i.e. the inner vectors are the elements.
  • each on a string: VERIFY — believed to splice per-character in 2021.01 ([each "ab"] == ["a","b"]) but version coverage is uncertain; probe, since it differs from concat (which treats a string as a single element).
  • each on a scalar/boolean/undef yields that value as one element: [each 5] == [5], [each undef] == [undef] — VERIFY undef case.
  • each on an empty vector or empty range contributes nothing.
  • each expanding a range MATERIALIZES it — [each [0:1e9]] is a resource hazard; ranges elsewhere stay lazy.
  • each [b:e] with reversed bounds inherits the range's deprecation-swap behavior — VERIFY warning emission in this context.
  • each is only meaningful inside [ ] literals/comprehension chains; 'x = each v;' outside brackets is a syntax error.
  • each is a keyword (2019.05+): scripts using 'each' as an identifier break — part of the 2019.05 compat delta.
  • Chained clause form 'for (...) each expr' is the standard flatten idiom; 'if (c) each v' splices conditionally.
echo([each [1, 2], each [3:5], each 6]); // ECHO: [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]
Implementer: Implement as an element-sink adapter shared by literal and comprehension contexts. Probe string/undef splicing against the oracle before freezing; libraries use each heavily for concat-free appends.

for (C-style comprehension clause)

syntax 2019.05 
for (init1 [, init2...] ; condition ; update1 [, update2...]) element-producing-rest

C-style generator (2019.05+), available ONLY inside comprehensions: initializes bindings, then repeatedly emits elements while the condition holds, applying the update assignments between iterations.

initNComma-separated name=expr bindings evaluated once before the loop; VERIFY sequential visibility (init2 seeing init1), believed sequential like let.
conditionExpression tested (standard truthiness) before EACH iteration including the first; false stops the loop.
updateNComma-separated name=expr assignments producing the next iteration's bindings. All update RHS expressions are evaluated against the CURRENT iteration's values, then applied together (simultaneous update) — this is what makes the canonical Fibonacci example work. VERIFY simultaneity explicitly.

Bindings live in a clause-local scope covering condition, updates, and the rest of the chain. Loop: test condition; if truthy, evaluate the rest of the chain (contributing elements), then compute updates and repeat. Updates may introduce ONLY names already in the init list — VERIFY whether updating a name not initialized is legal. The statement-level for module has NO such form; using semicolons in a for statement header is a syntax error.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Canonical probe: [for (a=0, b=1; a<10; a=b, b=a+b) a] must yield [0,1,1,2,3,5,8] — sequential (new-a) updates would wrongly give [0,1,2,4,8]; clone the simultaneous-update semantics bug-for-bug. VERIFY against oracle.
  • Condition false initially: result contributes zero elements, no warning.
  • Non-terminating condition hangs the evaluator — VERIFY: believed NO built-in iteration cap in 2021.01 (unlike range element limits); decide whether to add an opt-in guard without changing default behavior.
  • Multiple updates and multiple inits are comma-separated; a trailing comma is a syntax error — VERIFY.
  • undef flowing into the condition is falsy and terminates the loop silently; nan is TRUTHY and does NOT terminate it — a condition that degrades to nan loops until another bound stops it (typical of arithmetic-warning cascades).
  • The clause composes like any other: [for (i=0; i<3; i=i+1) if (i!=1) i] == [0,2].
  • $-names as loop bindings: VERIFY legality/dynamic behavior.
  • 2015.03 rejects this form with a syntax error — feature-test via version_num().
echo([for (a = 0, b = 1; a < 10; a = b, b = a + b) a]); // ECHO: [0, 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8]
Implementer: The Fibonacci probe is the single most important oracle test here — it pins update simultaneity. Also probe init sequentiality and updating-uninitialized-name behavior.

for (comprehension clause)

syntax 2015.03 
for (var1 = iterable1 [, var2 = iterable2, ...]) element-producing-rest

Generator clause: iterates exactly like the for statement (vector/range/string/scalar; multi-binding = cross product) but produces list elements instead of geometry.

varN = iterableNSame iterable forms and cross-product multi-binding as the for statement; bindings scope over the rest of the clause chain.
restThe remainder of the clause chain (further clauses or the element expression), evaluated once per iteration.

For each element of the iterable(s), evaluates the rest of the chain with the variable(s) bound; each evaluation contributes its elements (one for a plain expression, zero-or-more through if/each) to the enclosing vector in iteration order. Order is deterministic: source order for vectors/strings, arithmetic order for ranges, a-major for cross products.

Edge cases & oddities
  • All range quirks are shared with the for statement: reversed-range deprecation swap, step-0 warning, lazy expansion — VERIFY the step-0 warning text in expression context ('in expression' vs 'in for statement').
  • Scalar iterable yields one iteration: [for (i=5) i] == [5]; VERIFY [for (i=undef) i] (believed [undef], one iteration).
  • Strings iterate per code point (1-char strings), non-ASCII safe.
  • [for (a=[1,2], b=[10,20]) a+b] == [11,21,12,22] — cross product a-major; NOT zip.
  • The loop variable shadows outer names for the chain only.
  • $-variables may be bound as loop variables? VERIFY: for ($x = v) — believed legal and dynamically scoped; probe.
  • Nested successive for clauses ([for (a=v) for (b=a) b]) are equivalent to multi-binding only when the inner iterable doesn't depend on the outer variable; dependence requires the nested spelling.
echo([for (a = [1, 2], b = [10, 20]) a + b]); // ECHO: [11, 21, 12, 22]
Implementer: Share the iterable-expansion engine with the for statement verbatim so warning behavior stays identical; only the sink differs (list append vs child instantiation).

if / if-else (comprehension clause)

syntax 2015.03 
if (condition) element-producing-rest [else element-producing-rest]

Filter/branch clause: without else, iterations failing the condition contribute nothing (filtering); with else (2019.05+), one of two productions is chosen per element.

conditionStandard-truthiness expression, evaluated per surrounding iteration.
then/else productionRest-of-chain productions: a plain expression (one element), an each (splice), a let, a nested if, or a nested for.

Inside a chain, if guards everything to its right. The filter form is the only way to produce shorter-than-input lists from a single pass. Both arms may be full clause chains, so if (c) each a else each b and if (c) for (...) ... else ... compose. The untaken arm is not evaluated.

Edge cases & oddities
  • else requires 2019.05+; on 2015.03 only the filter form exists.
  • Dangling else binds to the nearest if within the chain — VERIFY with nested if/if/else probes.
  • A lone if with no for is legal: [if (c) 1] is [1] or [] — the conditional-element idiom.
  • Filtered-out iterations leave no placeholder (no undef).
  • VERIFY grammar corner: 'if (c) x else each y' and 'if (c) each x else y' both parse (mixed splice arms).
  • Condition sees all bindings from enclosing for/let clauses; undef conditions (e.g. from warned arithmetic) filter silently.
  • This clause is unrelated to the ternary; inside the ELEMENT expression itself you must use ?: (an if there is a syntax error).
echo([for (i = [0:5]) if (i % 2 == 0) i else -i]); // ECHO: [0, -1, 2, -3, 4, -5]
Implementer: Filter form is 2015.03, else form is 2019.05 — gate by reported version if you emulate old versions. Pin dangling-else association with oracle probes.

let (comprehension clause)

syntax 2015.03 
let (name1 = expr1 [, ...]) element-producing-rest

Per-element bindings inside a comprehension chain: sequential let bindings scoped over the remainder of the chain.

nameN = exprNSequential bindings (each sees earlier ones), re-evaluated once per surrounding iteration; may reference enclosing for/let bindings; $-names allowed.
restRemainder of the clause chain evaluated with the bindings in scope.

Identical binding semantics to the let expression, positioned as a chain clause: [for (x=v) let (y = f(x), z = g(x,y)) if (z) y] computes intermediates once per element. Purely lexical; contributes no elements itself.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Bindings recompute every iteration — hoist loop-invariant work OUTSIDE the comprehension for performance; a compliant implementation must not observably cache (echo-expression in a binding fires per iteration).
  • Sequentiality matters: let (a=1, b=a+1) — b sees a; VERIFY duplicate-name behavior matches let-expression.
  • let directly before the element expression (no for) is legal: [let (a=1) a] == [1].
  • $-bindings here dynamically affect function calls in the rest of the chain.
  • Shadowing an enclosing loop variable is legal and scoped to the chain remainder.
echo([for (x = [1, 2, 3]) let (sq = x * x) sq + 1]); // ECHO: [2, 5, 10]
Implementer: Same environment machinery as let-expression; no special cases beyond chain plumbing.

list comprehension

syntax 2015.03 
[ clause-chain ]  where clause := for(...) | for(init;cond;update) | if(cond) [else] | let(...) | each expr | expr

Vector-building expressions: inside a vector literal, generator clauses (for / C-style for / if / if-else / let / each) chain and nest arbitrarily, each chain terminating in an element expression; the result is always a vector.

clause-chainAny composition of the clause forms; each clause governs everything to its right until the chain terminates in an expression (or an each expr).

Clauses compose right-associatively: [for (x=v) if (p(x)) let (y=f(x)) each g(y)] iterates, filters, binds, and splices. Successive for clauses (or multiple bindings in one for) nest. The comprehension is an ordinary expression usable anywhere. Since 2019.05, generator clauses may be MIXED with plain literal elements inside one vector literal: [0, each v, for (i=r) i, 9] — plain elements contribute themselves in order, clauses contribute zero or more elements in place.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Empty productions collapse cleanly: [for (i=[]) i] is [], [if (false) 1] is [] — a comprehension may consist of a lone if clause with no for.
  • verify vs oracle exact grammar corners of mixing literals with clauses in 2021.01 ([0, for(i=r) i] believed legal since 2019.05; pin whether a leading clause plus trailing literals, nested mixed literals, and trailing commas all parse).
  • A chain must end in exactly one element expression; parenthesize commas ([(a, b)] is a syntax error — no tuple expressions).
  • The element expression may itself be a vector literal containing further clauses (nesting builds nested vectors; use each to flatten one level).
  • Comprehensions over ranges expand element-by-element and DO materialize the result vector — [each [0:1e8]] is an OOM/time hazard by design.
  • Result vectors are heterogeneous-friendly; skipped if-clauses leave no hole (no undef padding).
  • String iteration in comprehension for yields 1-character strings: [for (c="ab") c] == ["a","b"].
  • Comprehension clauses exist ONLY inside [ ] vector literals — there is no generator expression outside brackets.
  • 2015.03 baseline supports [for (...) expr] and let; each, C-style for, and if/else (the else part) require 2019.05.
v = [0, each [1, 2], for (i = [3:4]) i]; echo(v); // ECHO: [0, 1, 2, 3, 4]
Implementer: Parse clauses as a right-nested tree inside vector literals; evaluation appends to one output buffer (must be O(1) amortized append). Golden-test the mixed literal/clause grammar matrix against the oracle before freezing the parser.

Modules, functions, children

child (deprecated)

module pre-2015.03 (deprecated 2014.03; still functional in 2021.01; removed post-2021.01) 
child(); child(index);

Legacy singular predecessor of children(). Deprecated when children() arrived (2014.03), but STILL registered and functional in 2021.01; removed only after 2021.01.

index0-based index of the passed child to instantiate; omitted = first child.

Pre-2014 modules re-instantiated their passed subtree with child(). children() replaced it with range/index support in 2014.03. In 2021.01 child() still works: each call instantiates the indexed child and emits "DEPRECATED: child() will be removed in future releases. Use children() instead." Actual removal happened only after 2021.01.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Functional in 2021.01 with a per-call DEPRECATED message — VERIFY the exact text and whether it repeats per call or is deduplicated.
  • Migration is mechanical: child() -> children(0) / children(i).
// legacy: child(0);  — modern equivalent:
module pair(){ children(0); translate([10,0,0]) children(0); }
pair() circle(2);
Implementer: Implement as a deprecated alias of children() emitting the deprecation warning — 2021.01 parity requires it to work.

children

module 2014.03 
children(); | children(index); | children([i0, i1, ...]); | children([start:end]);

Inside a module body, instantiates the child statements attached at the call site: all of them, a single 0-based index, a vector of indices, or a range of indices; each children() call re-instantiates (children can be stamped repeatedly under different transforms).

(none)children() with no argument instantiates all call-site children in order.
index0-based single child index (number).
vector of indiceschildren([0,2]) instantiates exactly those children, in the vector's order.
rangechildren([0:2]) or [b:s:e]; each yielded index instantiates that child.

Children are passed lazily: the call-site block is not evaluated until a children() call instantiates it, and every children() call re-evaluates the selected children afresh. Children evaluate in the CALLER's lexical scope (they see the caller's variables, not the module's locals) but under the CALLEE's current dynamic $-environment (so a module can set $fn and its children see it). children() is only meaningful inside a module body; $children gives the count.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Out-of-range or negative index: warning approx 'WARNING: Children index (n) out of bounds (m children)' and that selection contributes nothing — VERIFY exact text and whether it is a warning or error.
  • Non-integer index: VERIFY (believed warning + skip); non-number argument: warning + skip.
  • children() with zero call-site children: no-op, no warning.
  • children([]) (empty vector) instantiates nothing.
  • Vector/range indices are each bounds-checked individually; a mixed valid/invalid vector instantiates the valid ones — VERIFY.
  • Multiple children() calls (or one inside a for) stamp the children multiple times — idiomatic; each stamping re-runs child evaluation (echo inside a child fires per stamping — VERIFY).
  • children() at top level / outside any module: error or warning — VERIFY text ('children() called outside module scope' approx).
  • Legacy child(i) still works in 2021.01 as a deprecated builtin: it instantiates the indexed child and emits 'DEPRECATED: child() will be removed in future releases. Use children() instead.' (removed only after 2021.01).
  • Scope rule probe: module m(){ x=99; children(); } x=1; m() echo(x); must print 1 (caller's lexical scope), while module m(){ $q=5; children(); } m() echo($q); prints 5 (callee's $-env).
  • Range index with reversed bounds inherits range deprecation-swap semantics — VERIFY in this position.
module twice() { children(0); translate([3, 0, 0]) children(0); } twice() cube(1);
Implementer: Represent children as an unevaluated closure over (caller lexical scope, per-call $-env). The scope/$-env split is what BOSL2 depends on — golden-test both probes above. Pin the out-of-bounds diagnostic verbatim.

function (named definition)

syntax pre-2015.03 
function name(p1, p2 = default, ...) = expression;

Defines a named function whose entire body is one expression; lives in the FUNCTION namespace, hoisted within its scope; recursion (with ternary as terminator) is the only looping construct.

nameIdentifier in the function namespace — may coexist with a module and a variable of the same name.
p1, p2 = defaultSame parameter semantics as modules: defaults evaluated at call time, may reference earlier parameters and outer scope; missing non-defaulted arguments bind undef.
expressionThe body and return value; a single expression (use let/ternary for structure). Terminated by ';'.

Callable before its textual definition (hoisting). Nested function definitions inside module bodies are lexically scoped. Calls evaluate arguments, bind via the standard matching rules, extend the definition's lexical environment, and evaluate the body; $-variables inside the body resolve DYNAMICALLY from the call site's $-environment. A named function is NOT a first-class value in 2021.01 — referring to 'name' as a variable hits the variable namespace instead (usually undef+warning); wrap it: g = function(x) name(x);.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Calling an undefined function: warning approx 'WARNING: Ignoring unknown function 'foo'' and the call yields undef; evaluation continues — VERIFY text.
  • Duplicate definition in one scope warns, last wins — VERIFY text.
  • User definitions shadow builtin functions (function sin(x) = 0;) — VERIFY precedence and whether any builtins are protected.
  • Ternary laziness is the recursion terminator; && and || short-circuit throughout the reference window (verified back to 2015.03) — pre-2019.05-style guards relied solely on ?:.
  • Tail-call elimination since 2015.03 for the core self-recursive-ternary pattern (2019.05 at most generalized it): self- and mutual tail calls in ternary branches (and through let — VERIFY let-tail coverage) run to millions of iterations; non-tail recursion is depth-bounded with 'ERROR: Recursion detected calling function 'name'' — see recursion entry.
  • Arguments are evaluated eagerly, exactly once, left to right at the call site — VERIFY order observability via echo-expression arguments.
  • A function call in statement position is a syntax error (no expression statements); results must feed assignments, arguments, or conditions.
  • Function parameters and body form a child scope; body cannot see call-site locals (lexical capture of the DEFINITION site + dynamic $-env only).
  • echo()/assert() expression forms are the only tracing/validation tools inside a function body (2019.05+).
function fact(n) = n <= 1 ? 1 : n * fact(n - 1); echo(fact(6)); // ECHO: 720
Implementer: Keep the three namespaces strictly separate. TCE is mandatory for BOSL2-class libraries; implement the evaluator with an explicit heap stack (wasm shadow stacks are tiny). Pin unknown-function/duplicate-definition warning texts.

group

module pre-2015.03 (internal, callable) 
group() { ... }

Undocumented but real builtin: a pure grouping node that implicitly unions its children — the node OpenSCAD itself emits in .csg dumps for every block.

Semantically identical to an anonymous union of its children. Every { } child block, module body, and loop body appears as group() in exported .csg trees, so a .csg-diffing test harness must both emit and accept it. It is callable from user source, where it behaves as union() without being one (VERIFY: interaction with intersection_for and CSG normalization — believed identical to union).

Edge cases & oddities
  • Not in the documentation or cheat sheet; presence is observable via .csg export.
  • verify vs oracle group() with modifier characters and in difference() subtrahend position — believed to behave exactly as union().
group(){ cube(1); sphere(1); } // same as union()
Implementer: Implement as an alias of union and EMIT it in .csg dumps where OpenSCAD does — the .csg golden-diff harness depends on matching this wrapper structure.

module (definition and instantiation)

syntax pre-2015.03 
module name(p1, p2 = default, ...) statement-or-{block}   /   name(args) [;| { children }]

Defines a named, parameterized geometry producer; instantiation runs the body with matched arguments, implicitly unioning all geometry the body emits; call-site children are accessible via children().

nameIdentifier in the MODULE namespace (independent of function and variable namespaces).
p1, p2 = defaultParameters; a parameter without a default that receives no argument binds undef (with a warning — VERIFY whether 2021.01 warns on missing non-defaulted args or binds silently). Defaults are expressions evaluated at call time and may reference EARLIER parameters and outer lexical scope.
bodyA block (or single statement) containing assignments, nested module/function definitions, and geometry statements; the body is a child scope; all emitted geometry is implicitly unioned.

Definitions are hoisted within their scope: a module may be instantiated textually before its definition. Nested definitions are lexically scoped to the enclosing body. Instantiation creates a fresh scope, binds parameters via the argument-matching rules, sets $children, pushes the module onto the instantiation stack (parent_module/$parent_modules), and evaluates the body. The call site may attach children with { } or a single trailing statement; children are lazily re-instantiated by each children() call, evaluating in the CALLER's lexical scope but under the CALLEE's current $-variable environment. Modules are not values: they cannot be assigned, passed, or returned (no module literals in any stable release).

Edge cases & oddities
  • Calling an undefined module: warning approx 'WARNING: Ignoring unknown module 'foo'' and the statement (including its children) is skipped — VERIFY text and that children are not evaluated.
  • Duplicate definition of the same module name in one scope: warning, last definition wins — VERIFY text (approx 'WARNING: module foo() was redefined').
  • User definitions SHADOW builtin modules (module cube() {...} hijacks cube) — VERIFY precedence; note the builtin becomes unreachable from inside (a self-call recurses).
  • Definitions are hoisted but their captured lexical environment is the whole scope (last-write-wins applies to variables they read).
  • A module body with zero geometry statements is legal and yields an empty group.
  • Passing children to a module that never calls children(): silently dropped, no warning in 2021.01 — VERIFY; but passing children to a builtin leaf primitive warns approx 'WARNING: module cube() does not support child modules' — VERIFY text and which builtins emit it.
  • Recursion is legal; depth-limited with 'ERROR: Recursion detected calling module 'name'' — see recursion entry.
  • Module names share the identifier lexicon; 'module for()' etc. are syntax errors only where the word is a reserved keyword (for, if, let, each, module, function, include, use, true, false, undef) — VERIFY the reserved set.
  • $-parameters may be declared as formals (module m($fn=8)) — VERIFY interaction: declared $-formal takes the passed value AND re-broadcasts dynamically to the subtree.
  • Semicolon after the closing brace of a definition is not required; an instantiation without children requires ';' or a child block.
module ring(r = 5) { difference() { circle(r); circle(r - 1); } } ring(8);
Implementer: Model instantiation as tree-building only (no geometry values). Cache evaluated subtrees keyed by module+arguments+$-env — OpenSCAD's cache is why 1000x loops finish. Pin all warning texts and the shadowing precedence of builtins via oracle probes.

Function literals (2021.01)

function literal

syntax 2021.01 
function (p1, p2 = default, ...) expression

Anonymous first-class function value (NEW in 2021.01): an expression producing a callable closure; supports default parameters; captures its lexical environment; $-variables inside the body still resolve dynamically at call time.

p1, p2 = defaultParameter list with the same syntax and matching rules as named functions (positional/named/$-args, defaults referencing earlier parameters).
expressionBody expression evaluated on call in the captured definition environment extended with the arguments.

Evaluating the literal yields a value of the distinct 'function' type: storable in variables and vectors, passable as arguments, returnable from functions — enabling user-space map/filter/fold (there are no builtins for those). The closure captures the lexical scope at the definition site; since bindings are immutable, capture-by-environment and capture-by-value are indistinguishable EXCEPT for $-variables, which are looked up dynamically in the CALLER's $-environment at each call, not the definition's.

Edge cases & oddities
  • A named 'function f(x) = ...' is NOT automatically a value: is_function(f) sees the VARIABLE f (usually undef + unknown-variable warning); the idiom is g = function(x) f(x); — no bare function references exist in 2021.01.
  • Self-recursion through the storing variable works: fac = function(n) n <= 0 ? 1 : n * fac(n - 1); — the body looks up 'fac' at call time in the captured scope where the slot exists — VERIFY this works in 2021.01 including under the assignment-slot-reordering rule.
  • echo of a function value prints a source-like rendering approx 'function(x, y = 1) ...' — VERIFY exact text (parameter list formatting, whether the body prints); str() of a function value follows the same rendering — VERIFY.
  • $-dynamic probe: f = function() $q; module m() { $q = 5; echo(f()); } m(); prints 5 — the definition site had no $q. Clone exactly; BOSL2 callbacks depend on it.
  • Function values in operators: f + 1, f == g, f < g — VERIFY (believed undef for arithmetic/relational; == on functions believed false unless — VERIFY — identical value); function values are truthy — VERIFY.
  • Function type is distinct: is_function true, is_num/is_list/is_string/is_bool false; len(f) undef; f[0] undef.
  • Literals appear anywhere expressions do: in vectors [function(x) x], as defaults (function g(h = function(x) x) = h(1)), in ternaries; precedence: the body extends maximally right — parenthesize when embedding, e.g. (function(x) x + 1)(2) — VERIFY grammar extent.
  • On 2019.05 and earlier the literal syntax is a hard SYNTAX ERROR (file fails to parse entirely) — feature-gate whole files, not call sites; libraries isolate 2021.01 code in separate files for this reason.
  • Default parameter expressions are evaluated at CALL time in the closure environment (earlier params visible) — same rules as named functions.
  • verify vs oracle tail-call elimination applies to recursive calls through function-value variables (believed yes).
add = function (a, b = 1) a + b; echo(add(2), add(2, 5)); // ECHO: 3, 7
Implementer: Represent as closure {param list, body AST, captured env ref}; $-lookup must bypass the captured env and use the caller's dynamic chain. Pin the echo rendering text and the equality/ordering behavior of function values via oracle probes — user code pattern-matches echo output in tests.

is_function

function 2021.01 
is_function(value)

Type predicate (2021.01): true iff the argument is a function value (a function literal / closure); false for every other type.

valueAny value.

Returns true only for values of the function type introduced in 2021.01. Completes the is_* family (is_undef/is_num/is_bool/is_string/is_list from 2019.05). Never warns, never errors, total over all values.

Edge cases & oddities
  • is_function(f) where f is a NAMED function definition: resolves f in the VARIABLE namespace — typically 'unknown variable' warning + undef + result false; named definitions are not values.
  • is_function on undef/numbers/strings/vectors/ranges/booleans: false, silently.
  • is_function is itself a builtin FUNCTION, not a keyword — VERIFY shadowability by a user definition.
  • On 2019.05: is_function does not exist — calling it warns unknown-function and yields undef (falsy), so naive feature probes accidentally 'work'; version_num() >= 20210100 is the reliable gate.
  • verify vs oracle is_function(is_function) — the bare name again hits the variable namespace (undef + warning + false), NOT true.
f = function (x) x * 2; echo(is_function(f), is_function(5)); // ECHO: true, false
Implementer: Trivial type-tag check; the interesting compat surface is the variable-namespace lookup of the argument (warnings included).

Operators, literals, lexical structure

! logical not

operator pre-2015.03 
!x

Truthiness negation; always returns a boolean.

xAny value, judged by truthiness.

Returns true if x is falsy, false if truthy. Falsy values: false, 0 (and -0), "", [], undef — five values; nan is truthy, so !(0/0) is false. Everything else is truthy, including "false", "0", [0], [[]], negative numbers, ranges, and function values.

Edge cases & oddities
  • !undef == true; !(0/0) == false (nan is truthy — the truthiness test is a plain != 0) — no warning for either.
  • !"false" == false (non-empty string is truthy).
  • ![] == true but ![[]] == false.
  • !range: a range is truthy (VERIFY: including degenerate ranges).
  • Never yields undef; output is strictly boolean.
echo(!0, !"", ![0], !undef); // ECHO: true, true, false, true

% modulo

operator pre-2015.03 
a % b

Floating-point remainder with C fmod semantics; result sign follows the dividend.

aDividend (number).
bDivisor (number).

Computes fmod(a,b): the remainder of truncating division; result has the sign of a (or is zero). Works on fractional operands: 5.5 % 2 == 1.5. Numbers only.

Edge cases & oddities
  • -7 % 3 == -1 and 7 % -3 == 1 (sign follows dividend) - NOT Python's floored modulo; libraries implement positive mod themselves.
  • x % 0 -> nan; VERIFY whether the division-by-zero WARNING is emitted for %.
  • inf % x -> nan; x % inf -> x (fmod semantics).
  • vector % anything: undef with WARNING.
  • Non-numeric operands: undef with WARNING.
  • There is no integer division; users write floor(a/b).
echo(-7%3, 5.5%2); // ECHO: -1, 1.5

&& and || logical and/or

operator pre-2015.03 
a && b, a || b

Short-circuiting logical operators returning booleans, not operands.

aLeft operand, judged by truthiness.
bRight operand, evaluated only if needed (short-circuiting holds throughout the reference window; verified back to 2015.03).

a && b: if a is falsy, result is false WITHOUT evaluating b; else result is the truthiness of b as a boolean. a || b: if a is truthy, result is true without evaluating b; else truthiness of b. Both return true/false, never the operand value (unlike JS/Python).

Edge cases & oddities
  • Short-circuiting holds throughout this document's reference window: verified in the 2015.03 source (C++ bool && / || on the evaluated left operand), and the 2014.03 release notes record if()-condition short-circuiting as fixed. Any non-short-circuit behavior is pre-2014 ancient history.
  • Return value is boolean: (5 && 7) == true, not 7 (VERIFY per seed).
  • undef && anything == false, no warning from the && itself.
  • Side effects in the skipped operand (echo-expression, assert-expression) do not fire - observable laziness.
  • && binds tighter than ||: a || b && c == a || (b && c).
  • No 'and'/'or' keywords; no xor operator.
echo(true || assert(false) 1); // ECHO: true (assert never evaluated)
Implementer: Laziness must extend through assert/echo expression operands; pin the boolean-not-operand return with echo(1&&2).

* multiplication

operator pre-2015.03 
a * b

Shape-dispatched: scalar product, scalar-vector scaling, vector dot product, and matrix products.

aNumber, vector, or matrix (vector of equal-length numeric row vectors).
bNumber, vector, or matrix conforming to a.

Dispatch by operand shapes: num*num -> num; num*vector or vector*num -> elementwise scale (recursive, so num*matrix scales every entry); vector*vector -> DOT PRODUCT (scalar; equal lengths required); matrix*vector -> vector (rows dot v); vector*matrix -> vector (v dot columns); matrix*matrix -> matrix product. Matrices are row-major nested lists.

Edge cases & oddities
  • vector*vector returning a scalar is the classic trap - elementwise multiply does not exist; users write comprehensions.
  • Length/dimension mismatch: undef with WARNING (VERIFY text).
  • Ragged 'matrices' (rows of unequal length): undef/WARNING - VERIFY exact behavior.
  • bool*num, string*num: undef with WARNING.
  • Empty vectors: []*[] -> 0 (empty dot product) - VERIFY; matrix forms with empty dims - VERIFY.
  • Mixed content vectors ([1,"a"]*[1,2]): undef with WARNING.
  • No implicit transpose anywhere; v*m treats v as a row vector.
echo([1,2,3]*[4,5,6], [[1,0],[0,2]]*[3,4]); // ECHO: 32, [3, 8]
Implementer: Pin dispatch order and every mismatch case via oracle; BOSL2 leans on all matrix forms.

.x .y .z member access

operator pre-2015.03 
vector.x, vector.y, vector.z

Swizzle sugar for the first three vector elements; pre-2015.03.

vectorA vector value; .x/.y/.z read elements 0/1/2.

v.x == v[0], v.y == v[1], v.z == v[2]. Works on any vector, including nested access like m[1].z. Read-only (no assignment targets).

Edge cases & oddities
  • In 2021.01 member access supports .x/.y/.z on vectors AND .begin/.step/.end on RANGE values (returning the range components); every other member name — or any non-vector/non-range receiver — yields undef silently, with no warning or error.
  • Short vector: [1].y -> undef (out of range).
  • On non-vector, non-range receivers (string, number, boolean, undef): undef, silently — per the silent-undef rule above. (Ranges are NOT in this bucket: they expose .begin/.step/.end.)
  • Snapshot 'object' values (textmetrics) extend dot access to arbitrary keys - snapshot-only.
p=[3,4,5]; echo(p.x+p.y, p.z); // ECHO: 7, 5

/ division

operator pre-2015.03 
a / b

Numeric division; vector/scalar divides elementwise; division by zero yields inf/nan with a warning.

aNumber or (recursively numeric) vector.
bNumber divisor.

num/num is IEEE division. vector/num divides each element, and num/vector divides the scalar by each element (both recursive into nested vectors, so matrix/scalar works and 10/[2,5] yields [5,2]). vector/vector and non-numeric shapes are undef with WARNING.

Edge cases & oddities
  • 1/0 -> inf, -1/0 -> -inf, each with 'WARNING: division by zero' (VERIFY exact text/format).
  • 0/0 -> nan with the same division-by-zero WARNING (VERIFY whether warning fires for 0/0).
  • scalar/vector IS supported elementwise: 10/[2,5] == [5,2], recursing into nested vectors; only vector/vector division (and non-numeric operands) is undef with WARNING.
  • vector/vector: undef with WARNING (no elementwise divide).
  • [1,2]/0 -> [inf, inf] plus warning(s) - VERIFY warning count (per element vs once).
  • -0 results follow IEEE (1/-0 -> -inf).
echo(7/2, [2,4]/2, 1/0); // ECHO: 3.5, [1, 2], inf

< <= >= > relational

operator pre-2015.03 
a < b, a <= b, a >= b, a > b

Ordering is defined for any SAME-TYPE pair except function values and undef: numbers (IEEE), strings (code-point lexicographic), booleans (false < true), vectors (lexicographic, elementwise-recursive), ranges (begin, then step, then element count). Mixed-type pairs yield undef with a WARNING.

aAny value; ordering is defined when b has the same type (numbers, strings, booleans, vectors, ranges) — function values and undef are never ordered.
bSame type as a; mixed-type pairs yield undef with a WARNING.

Numbers compare by IEEE ordering. Strings compare lexicographically by Unicode code point (no locale, no normalization). Booleans order false < true. Vectors compare lexicographically, elementwise-recursively: the first unequal element pair decides; a strict prefix sorts before the longer vector; an incomparable element pair propagates undef (tagged 'in vector comparison at index i'). Ranges compare by begin, then step, then element count. MIXED-type operands, function-function, and undef-undef yield undef with a WARNING like 'undefined operation (number < string)' (VERIFY exact texts per pair). History (source-verified): 2015.03/2019.05 ordered numbers, strings, and booleans — including mixed bool/number pairs via numeric coercion — and silently returned FALSE for every unsupported pair (vectors, ranges, other mixes); undef was impossible as a relational result. 2021.01 added vector/range ordering, made unsupported pairs undef instead of false, and dropped the bool/number cross-type comparison (now undef). Library code often still avoids vector comparison for portability.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Comparisons involving nan are false (IEEE), not undef: nan<1, nan>=1 both false - VERIFY OpenSCAD matches C here rather than returning undef.
  • "Z" < "a" is true (code points 90 < 97); "abc" < "abd"; prefix < longer ("ab" < "abc").
  • [1] < [2] is true; [1,2] < [1,3] is true; [1] < [1,0] is true (strict prefix sorts first) — vectors ARE ordered lexicographically in 2021.01. Pre-2021 releases silently returned FALSE for vector comparisons (never undef), so portable library code avoids them.
  • false < true is true and true < false is false — booleans ARE ordered in 2021.01.
  • undef in a comparison propagates undef; combined with ?: this silently takes else branches - a classic silent-bug source to reproduce faithfully.
  • -0 < 0 is false (they are equal).
echo(2<3, "abc"<"abd", [1]<[2]); // ECHO: true, true, true

== and != equality

operator pre-2015.03 
a == b, a != b

Total, deep, structural equality across all types; mismatched types are simply unequal (false, not undef).

aAny value.
bAny value.

Returns true/false for any operand pair. Numbers compare by IEEE ==; strings by exact code-point sequence; booleans by identity; vectors recursively (length then elementwise); undef==undef is true. Values of different types are unequal. != is the exact negation.

Edge cases & oddities
  • nan == nan is false and nan != nan is true (even to itself); a vector containing nan is therefore never == to anything, including itself (VERIFY the vector-with-nan case).
  • true == 1 is false; "1" == 1 is false - zero coercion.
  • 0 == -0 is true (IEEE).
  • [1,2] == [1,2,3] is false; [] == [] is true; deep nesting compares fully.
  • Range equality: [1:2]==[1:2] - VERIFY (believed true via begin/step/end compare); range == list is false.
  • Function-literal equality (2021.01): VERIFY behavior (believed false/undef for distinct literals) - pin against oracle.
  • Never emits a warning; never returns undef (contrast with relational operators).
echo(undef==undef, [1,[2]]==[1,[2]], 0/0==0/0); // ECHO: true, true, false
Implementer: Two different comparison regimes coexist (== total, < partial); do not unify them.

?: ternary conditional

operator pre-2015.03 
cond ? then_expr : else_expr

Lazy conditional expression - the language's only recursion terminator.

condAny value, judged by truthiness.
then_exprEvaluated only when cond is truthy.
else_exprEvaluated only when cond is falsy.

Evaluates cond; evaluates and returns exactly one branch. The untaken branch is NEVER evaluated - this laziness is what allows recursive functions to terminate. Right-associative; lowest precedence.

Edge cases & oddities
  • An eager implementation infinite-loops every recursive library function - laziness is non-negotiable.
  • A cond of undef takes the else branch (falsy); a cond of nan takes the THEN branch (nan is truthy in 2021.01); no warning for either.
  • Nesting chains right: a?b:c?d:e == a?b:(c?d:e); there is no 'elif'.
  • A ternary in tail position is eligible for tail-call elimination (core pattern since 2015.03) - both branches are tail contexts.
  • No warning ever originates from ?: itself; warnings come from the evaluated branch only.
function fact(n) = n<=1 ? 1 : n*fact(n-1); echo(fact(10)); // ECHO: 3.6288e+6

^ exponentiation

operator 2021.01 
base ^ exponent

Power operator, new in 2021.01; right-associative; identical math to pow().

baseNumber.
exponentNumber.

Computes C pow(base, exponent). Right-associative: a^b^c == a^(b^c). Binds tighter than unary minus but looser than postfix call/index. Numbers only.

Edge cases & oddities
  • -2^2 == -4 (unary minus applied after ^) - VERIFY this precedence corner against oracle.
  • 0^0 == 1 (C pow convention).
  • (-8)^(1/3) -> nan (pow of negative base with non-integer exponent); (-8)^2 == 64.
  • 0^-1 -> inf; VERIFY whether a warning accompanies it (believed none - pure pow).
  • Non-numeric operand: undef with WARNING.
  • Scripts targeting <=2019.05 must use pow(); ^ is a syntax error there - a compat-mode consideration.
  • 1^inf == 1, x^0 == 1 even for x=nan? pow(nan,0)==1 per C - VERIFY OpenSCAD matches.
echo(2^10, 2^3^2, -2^2); // ECHO: 1024, 512, -4
Implementer: Route ^ and pow() through the same pow implementation so they can never diverge.

binary + and -

operator pre-2015.03 
a + b, a - b

Numeric addition/subtraction and recursive elementwise vector addition/subtraction; no broadcasting.

aNumber, or vector (recursively numeric/vector) matching b's shape.
bSame shape as a.

number+number is IEEE addition. vector+vector of EQUAL length adds elementwise, recursing into nested vectors (so matrix+matrix works). Everything else is undef with a WARNING.

Edge cases & oddities
  • scalar+vector, vector+scalar: undef with WARNING - there is NO broadcasting for +/-.
  • Unequal-length vectors: undef with WARNING (VERIFY: some historic versions truncated to the shorter; 2021.01 believed undef).
  • "a"+"b" is undef with WARNING - str() is the only concatenation.
  • true+1, undef+1: undef with WARNING; approximate text 'WARNING: undefined operation (bool + number)' (VERIFY exact wording/format).
  • inf and nan propagate per IEEE: inf+(-inf)=nan, no extra warning.
  • Nested mismatch ([1,[2]]+[1,2]) makes only the mismatched element undef or the whole result undef - VERIFY which; believed whole-result undef.
  • Warnings count toward --hardwarnings fatality.
echo([1,2]+[10,20], [1,2]+1); // ECHO: [11, 22], undef (with warning)
Implementer: Oracle-probe unequal lengths and nested mismatches; the warning text is compatibility surface.

boolean and undef literals

syntax pre-2015.03 
true, false, undef

Keyword literals for the two booleans and the undefined value.

true/false are the boolean type; undef is a distinct singleton type meaning 'no value'. undef is what missing parameters, failed lookups, out-of-range indexing, and type-mismatched operations produce.

Edge cases & oddities
  • true == 1 is false: no numeric coercion in ==; but both true and 1 are truthy in conditions.
  • undef == undef is true; undef compared relationally (<) is undef.
  • Operations on undef (undef+1 etc.) yield undef with a console WARNING; evaluation continues.
  • Bare use of a never-assigned variable warns ('WARNING: Ignoring unknown variable ...' - VERIFY exact text) and yields undef; is_undef(var) suppresses that warning.
  • They are keywords: 'true = 5;' is a syntax error.
echo(true, false, undef, true==1); // ECHO: true, false, undef, false

function call operator

operator pre-2015.03 
callee(positional..., name=value..., $special=value...)

Invokes a named function/builtin or (2021.01) any expression evaluating to a function value.

calleeFunction name, or any postfix expression yielding a function value (2021.01).
argumentsPositional first, then named in any order; $-named arguments always accepted and become dynamic specials for the call.

Arguments bind positionally then by name; a parameter given twice - VERIFY (warning, later wins?). Missing parameters without defaults become undef. Unknown named arguments WARN and are ignored, except $-prefixed ones which are always accepted. Defaults may reference earlier parameters. Expression-callees like funcs[i](x) or (pick(n))(x) are 2021.01.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Calling an undefined function name: WARNING ('WARNING: Ignoring unknown function ...' - VERIFY text) and undef.
  • Calling a non-function value ((5)(1)): WARNING + undef (VERIFY text).
  • Named-argument shadowing a positional already bound: VERIFY behavior (warning + which wins).
  • Tail calls are eliminated since 2015.03 (core self-recursive-ternary pattern; 2019.05 at most generalized it) - millions of frames deep must work; non-tail recursion hits a bounded stack with a 'Recursion detected' style error (pin depth + message).
  • $-arguments propagate dynamically to everything the callee evaluates.
  • Functions/modules/variables are three namespaces: f=5; function f(x)=x; both coexist.
function add(a,b=1)=a+b; echo(add(2), add(b=10,a=1)); // ECHO: 3, 11
Implementer: since=pre-2015.03 for named/positional binding; expression callees are 2021.01. Oracle-probe duplicate-binding and unknown-arg warning texts.

number literals

syntax pre-2015.03 
123, 1.5, .5, 1e-3, 2.5E+6

Numeric literals; all numbers are IEEE-754 double precision. There is no integer type.

Decimal digits with optional fraction and optional e/E exponent (exponent sign optional). A leading '.' form (.5) is accepted. Every number in the language is a C double; integer-looking values are doubles that happen to be integral. Literal value is the nearest double (standard strtod rounding).

Edge cases & oddities
  • No hex, octal, or binary literals - '0x10' is a parse error/lexes as 0 followed by identifier x10 (VERIFY: exact lexer behavior on 0x10).
  • No literal for inf or nan; they arise only from arithmetic (1/0, 0/0) - the identifiers 'inf'/'nan' are ordinary undefined variables.
  • Literals exceeding double range (1e999) become inf silently (strtod overflow).
  • verify vs oracle trailing-dot form '5.' accepted by the lexer.
  • Negative numbers are unary minus applied to a positive literal, not part of the literal - matters for precedence with ^.
  • -0 exists as a distinct double but echo prints it as 0 (UNIQUE_ZERO in 2021.01's double-conversion formatter); its sign is observable only via arithmetic such as 1/-0 == -inf.
echo(1e-3, .5, 2.5E+6, 1e999); // ECHO: 0.001, 0.5, 2.5e+6, inf
Implementer: Parse with strtod-equivalent; store f64 everywhere. Pin 0x, 5., and 1e999 behavior via oracle probes.

string literals

syntax pre-2015.03 
"text with \" \\ \t \n \r escapes"

Double-quoted UTF-8 string literals with a small escape set.

Strings are immutable sequences of Unicode code points stored as UTF-8. Only double quotes delimit strings. Recognized escapes: \" \\ \t \n \r, plus (since 2015.03) \x## (two hex digits, one byte), \u#### (4 hex digits) and \U###### (6 hex digits) for Unicode code points. Arbitrary UTF-8 (including astral characters) may appear directly in source.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Unicode/hex escapes \x## (hex byte), \u#### and \U###### (code points) are supported since 2015.03. VERIFY: behavior for invalid hex digits and out-of-range \U values — pin against oracle.
  • verify vs oracle unrecognized escape like \q - believed to keep the character (or backslash+char); pin against oracle.
  • No single-quoted strings; ' is a syntax error.
  • Unterminated string at EOF is a parse error.
  • A literal newline inside the quotes: VERIFY whether accepted verbatim or an error.
  • len/indexing count code points, not bytes; s[i] yields a 1-character string.
echo("a\tb\n\"q\""); // ECHO: "a	b
"q""
Implementer: Store UTF-8, index by code point. Oracle-probe unknown escapes and embedded newlines before freezing the lexer.

unary minus and plus

operator pre-2015.03 
-x, +x

Numeric negation; -vector negates elementwise recursively.

xNumber or recursively numeric vector.

-number negates; -vector negates every element recursively (so -matrix works). Precedence: below ^, above * / %.

Edge cases & oddities
  • -true, -"a", -undef: undef with WARNING.
  • -[1,"a"]: VERIFY - believed undef (or element-undef) with WARNING.
  • -0 is produced and is IEEE negative zero; echo display of -0 must be pinned (VERIFY).
  • Unary + : VERIFY whether the grammar accepts +x at all in 2021.01 (believed accepted as a no-op on numbers); pin it.
  • --x parses as -(-x) (two unary operators).
  • -2^2 is -(2^2) - see precedence.
echo(-[1,-2,[3]]); // ECHO: [-1, 2, [-3]]

v[i] indexing

operator pre-2015.03 
value[index]

Zero-based element access on vectors and strings; fractional indices truncate toward zero; negative, non-finite, out-of-range, or non-numeric indices yield undef.

valueVector or string.
indexNumber; must be an integral value in [0, len).

vector[i] returns the i-th element. string[i] returns the i-th code point as a 1-character string. Chains for matrices: m[1][2]. The index is converted by TRUNCATION toward zero (v[1.5] == v[1] — a uint32 cast with Trunc rounding, no integrality check); negative, non-finite (nan/inf), out-of-bounds, or non-numeric indices yield undef.

Edge cases & oddities
  • No negative indexing (v[-1] is undef, not last element) and no slicing.
  • Fractional in-range indices TRUNCATE toward zero: v[1.5] == v[1] (also for strings and range subscripts — r[1.5] is r[1] = step). Negative, nan, and inf indices convert out of bounds and yield undef.
  • range[0]/range[1]/range[2] return begin/step/end (2021.01; parallel to .begin/.step/.end member access); other range indices, and any number, boolean, or undef receiver -> undef.
  • Indexing a number, boolean, or undef receiver -> undef; range receivers define only indices 0-2 = begin/step/end (see the range bullet).
  • String indexing is by code point over UTF-8 - byte indexing corrupts non-ASCII.
  • v[true] -> undef (no bool-to-number coercion) - VERIFY.
  • v["x"]: undef - member-style access via brackets does not exist; use v.x.
v=[10,20,30]; s="abc"; echo(v[2], s[0], v[-1], v[1.5]); // ECHO: 30, "a", undef, 20

vector literals

syntax pre-2015.03 
[e0, e1, ...]

Bracketed, comma-separated, heterogeneous, arbitrarily nested list values - the only container type.

Evaluates each element expression left to right and produces a vector. Elements may be any mix of types including vectors (matrices are vectors of row vectors). [] is the empty vector. Inside a vector literal, generator clauses are also legal: 'each expr' splices, 'for(...) expr', 'if(...)...', and 'let(...) expr' comprehension clauses may be mixed with plain elements in 2021.01.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Trailing comma [1,2,] is tolerated (VERIFY: version that introduced tolerance; believed accepted in 2021.01).
  • [1, each [2,3], for(i=[4:5]) i] is legal 2021.01 and yields [1,2,3,4,5] (VERIFY grammar corners).
  • each on a range expands it; each on a non-list yields the value itself as one element.
  • [a:b] with a colon is a RANGE literal, not a 2-vector - the bracket syntax is overloaded.
  • Vectors compare with == deeply; there is no reference identity.
  • Comprehension result is always a fresh vector; empty generator yields [].
v=[1,"a",[2,3],true]; echo(v, len(v)); // ECHO: [1, "a", [2, 3], true], 4
Implementer: Comprehension clauses inside plain literals share one grammar; implement the vector literal as a list of element-producers.

Math builtins

abs

function pre-2015.03 
abs(x)

Absolute value of a number.

xNumber.

Returns |x| as a double (C fabs).

Edge cases & oddities
  • abs(-0) == 0.
  • abs(nan) == nan; abs(-inf) == inf.
  • Non-numeric argument (vector, string, bool, undef): undef with WARNING (approx 'abs() parameter could not be converted' - VERIFY text).
  • No arguments / missing x: undef with WARNING (VERIFY).
echo(abs(-3.5), abs(0/0)); // ECHO: 3.5, nan

acos

function pre-2015.03 
acos(x)

Arc cosine returning DEGREES in [0, 180].

xNumber in [-1, 1].

Angle in degrees whose cosine is x. acos(1)==0, acos(0)==90, acos(-1)==180.

Edge cases & oddities
  • |x|>1 -> nan with WARNING (2019.05+; VERIFY text).
  • acos(nan) -> nan.
  • Non-numeric: undef with WARNING.
  • acos(-1)==180 exactly (VERIFY).
echo(acos(-1), acos(0)); // ECHO: 180, 90

asin

function pre-2015.03 
asin(x)

Arc sine returning DEGREES in [-90, 90].

xNumber in [-1, 1].

Returns the angle in degrees whose sine is x. asin(1)==90, asin(0.5)==30, asin(-1)==-90.

Edge cases & oddities
  • |x|>1 -> nan, with WARNING in 2019.05+ (approx 'asin() out of range' - VERIFY text and presence).
  • asin(nan) -> nan.
  • Non-numeric: undef with WARNING.
  • Exactness: asin(1)==90 exactly, asin(0)==0 (VERIFY special-casing of results at quadrant values).
echo(asin(0.5), asin(2)); // ECHO: 30, nan

atan

function pre-2015.03 
atan(x)

Arc tangent returning DEGREES in (-90, 90).

xAny number.

Angle in degrees whose tangent is x. atan(1)==45.

Edge cases & oddities
  • atan(inf)==90, atan(-inf)==-90 (VERIFY exactness).
  • atan(nan) -> nan; non-numeric: undef with WARNING.
  • Cannot distinguish quadrants - atan2 exists for that.
echo(atan(1), atan(-1)); // ECHO: 45, -45

atan2

function pre-2015.03 
atan2(y, x)

Full-quadrant arc tangent of y/x, in DEGREES, range (-180, 180].

yOrdinate - FIRST argument.
xAbscissa - second argument.

Returns the angle of the point (x,y) in degrees using both signs to pick the quadrant: atan2(1,1)==45, atan2(1,-1)==135, atan2(-1,-1)==-135. Argument order is y then x (C convention).

Edge cases & oddities
  • atan2(0,0) == 0 (C behavior, no warning) - VERIFY.
  • Signed-zero corners follow C atan2: atan2(0,-1)==180, atan2(-0,-1)==-180 - VERIFY OpenSCAD preserves -0 distinctions.
  • atan2(y,x) with nan -> nan; non-numeric or missing arg: undef with WARNING.
  • Result exactly 90/180 at axis points (VERIFY exactness special-casing).
  • Degrees, unlike every other language's atan2.
echo(atan2(1,-1), atan2(-1,0)); // ECHO: 135, -90

ceil

function pre-2015.03 
ceil(x)

Smallest integral value >= x.

xNumber.

C ceil. ceil(2.1)==3, ceil(-2.9)==-2.

Edge cases & oddities
  • ceil(-0.5)==-0 (negative zero result from C ceil - VERIFY echo display).
  • ceil(nan)==nan, ceil(inf)==inf.
  • Non-numeric: undef with WARNING.
  • Used by the $fa/$fs fragment formula - must match C ceil bit-for-bit or tessellation counts drift.
echo(ceil(2.1), ceil(-2.9)); // ECHO: 3, -2

cos

function pre-2015.03 
cos(degrees)

Cosine of an angle in DEGREES with exact quadrant values.

degreesAngle in degrees.

cos in degrees; angles reducing to multiples of 90 return exact 0/1/-1: cos(90)==0 exactly, cos(180)==-1.

Edge cases & oddities
  • cos(90)==0 exactly (not 6.1e-17) - see sin for the exactness note; VERIFY introduction release.
  • cos(nan)/cos(inf) -> nan.
  • Non-numeric: undef with WARNING.
  • Hex-nut idiom depends on cos: r/cos(180/6); keep full double precision for non-special angles.
  • Same 30/45/60-in-every-quadrant exactness as sin: cos(60)==0.5 and cos(45)==sqrt(1/2) exactly (2019.05+).
echo(cos(60), cos(90)); // ECHO: 0.5, 0

cross

function 2015.03 (2D scalar form; 3D form pre-2015.03) 
cross(a, b)

Vector cross product: 3-vectors give a 3-vector; 2-vectors give the scalar z-component (2015.03+).

aNumeric vector of length 3 (or 2, since 2015.03).
bNumeric vector of the SAME length as a.

For 3-vectors returns [a1*b2-a2*b1, a2*b0-a0*b2, a0*b1-a1*b0]. For two 2-vectors returns the scalar a0*b1-a1*b0. Both arguments must have the same length, 2 or 3.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Length mismatch (3 with 2) or lengths other than 2/3: undef with WARNING (approx 'Invalid parameters for cross()' - VERIFY text).
  • 2D form since 2015.03 (returns the scalar v0[0]*v1[1] - v0[1]*v1[0]); only 2014.03-era releases warned/returned undef for 2-vectors.
  • Non-numeric elements: undef with WARNING (VERIFY vs nan propagation).
  • Missing second argument: undef with WARNING.
  • No n-ary or chained form; exactly two arguments.
echo(cross([1,0,0],[0,1,0]), cross([1,0],[0,1])); // ECHO: [0, 0, 1], 1
Implementer: since marks the material 2D change; 3D form is pre-2015.03.

exp

function pre-2015.03 
exp(x)

e raised to the power x.

xNumber.

C exp(x). exp(1) is the idiom for the missing E constant.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Overflow (exp(1000)) -> inf, silently.
  • exp(-inf)==0, exp(nan)==nan.
  • Non-numeric: undef with WARNING.
  • There is no E or TAU constant; only PI exists.
echo(exp(1), exp(0)); // ECHO: 2.71828, 1

floor

Largest integral value <= x.

xNumber.

ln

function pre-2015.03 
ln(x)

NATURAL logarithm (base e).

xNumber > 0.

C log(x). ln(exp(1))==1.

Edge cases & oddities
  • ln(0) -> -inf; VERIFY whether a warning is emitted (believed silent).
  • ln(negative) -> nan with WARNING in 2019.05+ (VERIFY text, approx 'ln() out of range').
  • ln(inf)==inf, ln(nan)==nan.
  • Non-numeric: undef with WARNING.

log

function pre-2015.03 
log(x)

BASE-10 logarithm (not natural).

xNumber > 0.

C log10(x). log(1000)==3.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Base 10, not e - the classic trap; there is no two-argument log(base,x) in 2021.01 (VERIFY - users write ln(x)/ln(b)).
  • log(0) -> -inf (VERIFY warning); log(negative) -> nan with WARNING (2019.05+).

echo(log(1000), log(0.01)); // ECHO: 3, -2

max

function pre-2015.03 (single-vector-argument form: 2015.03) 
max(a, b, ...) | max(vector)

Maximum of two-or-more arguments, or of a single vector's elements.

a, b, ...Two or more numbers.
vectorAlternative single vector argument.

Mirror of min with > ordering; identical dispatch rules.

Edge cases & oddities
  • max([]) -> WARNING + undef.
  • max(5) single scalar: VERIFY (believed WARNING + undef).
  • nan handling, string handling, mixed types: same VERIFY set as min - keep the two functions exactly symmetric.
  • Used pervasively in fragment sizing (cylinder uses max(r1,r2)) - numeric behavior must be exact.
  • The single-vector form (max([a,b,c])) was added in 2015.03; earlier releases warn/return undef for a vector argument. It compares with the generic relational less-than, so a vector of strings returns the lexicographic extreme (see string comparison).
echo(max(3,1,2), max([5,4,6])); // ECHO: 3, 6

min

function pre-2015.03 (single-vector-argument form: 2015.03) 
min(a, b, ...) | min(vector)

Minimum of two-or-more arguments, or of a single vector's elements.

a, b, ...Two or more numbers (VERIFY: strings also comparable - see edge cases).
vectorAlternative single argument: a vector whose elements are compared.

With >=2 arguments, returns the smallest; with exactly one vector argument, returns the smallest element. Comparison is the relational < ordering.

Edge cases & oddities
  • min([]) (empty vector): WARNING + undef (approx 'min() parameter could not be converted' or 'empty vector' - VERIFY text).
  • min(5) with a single scalar: VERIFY - believed WARNING + undef (one argument must be a vector).
  • Strings: VERIFY whether min("a","b") works lexicographically or is undef - pin against oracle.
  • Mixed/mismatched element types in the vector: undef with WARNING (VERIFY).
  • nan among arguments: result order-dependent under C comparisons - pin min(nan,1) and min(1,nan) against oracle (VERIFY).
  • Nested vectors as elements: VERIFY (believed undef - no deep min).
  • min(vector, scalar) two-arg mix: undef with WARNING (VERIFY).
  • The single-vector form (min([a,b,c])) was added in 2015.03; earlier releases warn/return undef for a vector argument. It compares with the generic relational less-than, so a vector of strings returns the lexicographic extreme (see string comparison).
echo(min(3,1,2), min([5,4,6])); // ECHO: 1, 4
Implementer: The 1-vector vs n-scalar dispatch and every degenerate case need oracle pinning; libraries call both forms.

norm

function pre-2015.03 
norm(v)

Euclidean length of a numeric vector of any dimension.

vVector of numbers (any length).

Returns sqrt(sum of squares of elements). Works for any dimension: norm([3,4])==5, norm([1,1,1,1])==2.

Edge cases & oddities
  • norm([]) == 0 (empty sum) - VERIFY.
  • Any non-numeric element (string, vector, undef, bool): undef with WARNING (VERIFY: whether nested numeric vectors are rejected - believed yes, no Frobenius norm).
  • norm of a string: VERIFY - believed undef (some docs mention strings; pin it).
  • norm(scalar) / norm(undef): undef with WARNING.
  • Elements of inf -> inf; nan element -> nan.
  • Overflow: naive sum-of-squares overflows around 1e154 -> inf; VERIFY OpenSCAD is naive (no hypot-style scaling) and clone that.
echo(norm([3,4]), norm([1,1,1,1])); // ECHO: 5, 2

PI

constant pre-2015.03 
PI

Built-in constant 3.14159265358979... (full double precision M_PI).

A predefined variable in the outermost scope holding pi as a double. Not a keyword - it is an ordinary (non-$) variable name.

Edge cases & oddities
  • echo(PI) prints 3.14159 (6-significant-digit %g formatting), but the stored value is full precision.
  • There is NO E constant (users write exp(1)) and no TAU.
  • User code CAN assign PI = 3; shadowing/overriding it - VERIFY whether a reassignment warning fires and whether builtins (nothing consumes PI internally - they use their own constant) are affected (they are not).
  • Case-sensitive: pi is an unknown variable (undef + warning).
echo(PI, 2*PI*10); // ECHO: 3.14159, 62.8319

pow

function pre-2015.03 
pow(base, exponent)

base raised to exponent; the pre-2021.01 spelling of ^.

baseNumber.
exponentNumber.

C pow(base, exponent). Identical math to the ^ operator (2021.01).

Edge cases & oddities
  • pow(0,0)==1; pow(0,-1)==inf; pow(-8, 1/3)==nan (negative base, non-integer exponent).
  • Missing exponent (pow(2)): undef with WARNING (VERIFY).
  • Non-numeric args: undef with WARNING.
  • Not deprecated in 2021.01 - both spellings must work forever; keep them byte-identical in behavior.
  • pow(nan,0)==1 per C - VERIFY OpenSCAD matches.

round

function pre-2015.03 
round(x)

Round to nearest integer, halves away from zero.

xNumber.

Nearest integral value; ties round away from zero: round(2.5)==3, round(-2.5)==-3, round(2.4)==2.

Edge cases & oddities
  • verify vs oracle half-away-from-zero via C round() - implementations differ (banker's rounding would give round(2.5)==2); pin round(0.5), round(-0.5), round(2.5), round(-2.5) against oracle.
  • round(-0.4)==-0 (VERIFY display).
  • round(nan)==nan, round(inf)==inf.

sign

function pre-2015.03 
sign(x)

Sign of a number as -1, 0, or 1.

xNumber.

Returns -1 for x<0, 1 for x>0, 0 for x==0. Result is a plain number, never +-0.

Edge cases & oddities
  • sign(-0) == 0.
  • sign(inf)==1, sign(-inf)==-1.
  • sign(nan): VERIFY - a comparison-based implementation returns 0; some return nan. Pin against oracle.
  • Non-numeric: undef with WARNING.

sin

function pre-2015.03 
sin(degrees)

Sine of an angle given in DEGREES.

degreesAngle in degrees (any magnitude; reduced mod 360).

Returns sin of the angle interpreted in degrees. OpenSCAD special-cases angles that reduce to multiples of 90 degrees so results are EXACT: sin(180)==0 (not 1.2e-16), sin(90)==1, sin(30)==0.5 exactly — 30, 45 and 60 are special-cased in every quadrant alongside the 0/90/180/270 folding (degree-exact trig, 2019.05+).

Edge cases & oddities
  • DEGREES, not radians - the single most common porting error.
  • Exact values not just at quadrants but at 30/45/60 in EVERY quadrant: sin(30)==0.5, sin(45)==sqrt(1/2), sin(60)==sqrt(3)/2 exactly; angle folded/reduced first, so sin(36000)==0 exactly. Present in 2019.05 and 2021.01 (post-dates 2015.03).
  • sin(inf), sin(nan) -> nan (VERIFY warning; believed silent nan).
  • Non-numeric: undef with WARNING.
  • Negative and >360 angles fine (periodic reduction).
  • |angle| >= 2^52 * 360 returns NaN for sin/cos/tan (deliberate total-precision-loss guard).
  • Inverse trig snaps to whole degrees when the whole-degree value round-trips: asin(1)==90 and acos(-1)==180 exactly.
echo(sin(30), sin(180)); // ECHO: 0.5, 0
Implementer: Implement as sin(deg2rad(fmod-reduced angle)) with exact-value special cases for quadrant multiples; oracle-probe sin(45), sin(1e10).

sqrt

function pre-2015.03 
sqrt(x)

Square root.

xNumber >= 0.

C sqrt(x).

Edge cases & oddities
  • sqrt(negative) -> nan with WARNING (2019.05+; VERIFY text, approx 'sqrt() out of range' or 'sqrt of negative').
  • sqrt(-0)==-0 (IEEE; VERIFY display).
  • sqrt(inf)==inf, sqrt(nan)==nan.
  • Non-numeric: undef with WARNING.
  • No cbrt builtin; users write pow(x,1/3) which is nan for negative x.
echo(sqrt(2), sqrt(-1)); // ECHO: 1.41421, nan

tan

function pre-2015.03 
tan(degrees)

Tangent of an angle in DEGREES.

degreesAngle in degrees.

tan in degrees; special-cased so tan(45)==1 and tan(180)==0 exactly.

Edge cases & oddities
  • tan(90) returns +inf and tan(270) returns -inf via explicit special case; tan(180) returns -0.0 internally — echo prints it as "0" (the 2021.01 formatter maps negative zero to "0"); the sign is observable only indirectly, e.g. 1/tan(180) == -inf.
  • tan(30)==1/sqrt(3), tan(45)==1, tan(60)==sqrt(3) exactly — 30/45/60 are special-cased in every quadrant (2019.05+).
  • tan(nan)/tan(inf) -> nan.
  • Non-numeric: undef with WARNING.
echo(tan(45), tan(180)); // ECHO: 1, 0

Type-test builtins

is_bool

function 2019.05 
is_bool(x)

True iff x is exactly true or false.

xAny value.

Type test for the boolean type. No truthiness involved: only the two boolean values pass.

Edge cases & oddities
  • is_bool(0)==false, is_bool(1)==false, is_bool("true")==false, is_bool(undef)==false.
  • Result of comparisons and !/&&/|| is boolean, so is_bool(1==1)==true.
  • No args: VERIFY.
echo(is_bool(false), is_bool(0)); // ECHO: true, false

is_list

function 2019.05 
is_list(x)

True iff x is a vector (including []); FALSE for strings and ranges.

xAny value.

Type test for the vector/list type. Nested and heterogeneous vectors pass; the empty vector passes.

Edge cases & oddities
  • is_list([])==true.
  • is_list("abc")==false - strings iterate like lists in comprehensions but are NOT lists.
  • is_list([0:5])==false - ranges are a distinct type; since there is no is_range, a range is only detectable by elimination (!is_list && !is_num && !is_string && !is_bool && !is_undef && !is_function).
  • is_list(undef)==false.
  • No args: VERIFY.
echo(is_list([]), is_list("ab"), is_list([0:2])); // ECHO: true, false, false
Implementer: The elimination-based range detection idiom appears in libraries - all six sibling tests must be consistent.

is_num

function 2019.05 
is_num(x)

True for finite and infinite numbers; FALSE for nan.

xAny value.

Returns true iff x is of number type AND is not nan. This nan exclusion is documented upstream behavior: is_num(0/0) is false.

Edge cases & oddities
  • is_num(0/0)==false - the nan carve-out is the trap; nan IS stored as a number but tests false.
  • is_num(1/0)==true - inf is believed to test TRUE (VERIFY against oracle, per seed).
  • is_num(-0)==true.
  • is_num(true)==false (no coercion), is_num("1")==false, is_num(undef)==false, is_num([1])==false, is_num(range)==false.
  • No args: VERIFY.
echo(is_num(3), is_num(0/0), is_num(1/0)); // ECHO: true, false, true
Implementer: Implement as type==number && !isnan(value); pin the inf case.

is_object

function snapshot 
is_object(x)

SNAPSHOT-ONLY: type test for the experimental object (dict-like) type returned by textmetrics()/fontmetrics().

xAny value.

Returns true iff x is an object value - a key/value structure with dot access, produced only by snapshot-gated features (--enable=textmetrics and later object literals in newer nightlies). Not present in 2021.01.

Edge cases & oddities
  • In 2021.01 calling is_object is an unknown-function WARNING + undef - a 2021.01-compat implementation should reproduce that unless snapshot features are opted in.
  • is_object([])==false, is_object(undef)==false - objects are distinct from vectors.
  • The object type itself is a moving snapshot target (object literals, has_key etc. in 2024+ nightlies) - VERIFY per-snapshot if chasing snapshot parity.
  • Decide-and-document: implement behind an --enable flag mirroring upstream, or omit with the faithful unknown-function warning.
// snapshot only, --enable=textmetrics
tm = textmetrics("hi"); echo(is_object(tm)); // ECHO: true

is_string

function 2019.05 
is_string(x)

True iff x is a string.

xAny value.

Type test for strings, including "".

Edge cases & oddities
  • is_string("")==true.
  • is_string(str(5))==true - str always yields a string.
  • A 1-character indexing result s[0] is a string -> true; there is no char type.
  • is_string(["a"])==false; is_string(undef)==false.
  • No args: VERIFY.
echo(is_string(""), is_string(5)); // ECHO: true, false

is_undef

function 2019.05 
is_undef(x)

True iff x is undef; uniquely suppresses the unknown-variable warning, acting as a defined() guard.

xAny expression; a bare never-defined variable name is probed silently.

Returns true when the argument evaluates to undef, false otherwise. SPECIAL: is_undef(foo) where foo was never assigned returns true WITHOUT the usual 'unknown variable' WARNING that a bare reference would emit - the documented way to test for optional/overridable variables.

Edge cases & oddities
  • The warning suppression scope: VERIFY whether it covers only a bare variable argument or any expression (is_undef(foo+1) - believed the lookup inside still warns; pin it).
  • is_undef(undef)==true; is_undef(0)==false, is_undef(nan)==false, is_undef([])==false, is_undef(false)==false.
  • A missing function parameter (no default, not passed) is undef -> true.
  • v[99] out-of-range feeds is_undef true - the idiom for end-of-list in recursion.
  • No arguments: VERIFY (error vs true).
  • Introduced 2019.05; before that, scripts compared x==undef (which also works, since == is total).
echo(is_undef(never_set), is_undef(0)); // ECHO: true, false
Implementer: Implement the silent-lookup special case in the evaluator, not the function - it changes warning emission, which --hardwarnings turns fatal.

String builtins and string semantics

chr

function 2015.03 
chr(n, ...) -> string  where each argument is a number, a vector of numbers, or a range

Converts Unicode code point number(s) to a string; multiple arguments, vector arguments and range arguments all concatenate.

n, ...Each argument: a code point number, a vector of code point numbers, or a range of code points. All resulting characters are concatenated in argument order.

Each numeric value is interpreted as a Unicode code point and converted to the corresponding character; all characters from all arguments are concatenated into one string encoded as UTF-8. Vector arguments contribute one character per element; range arguments are expanded and contribute one character per step. Invalid code points contribute nothing (empty string) rather than erroring.

Edge cases & oddities
  • chr(65) == "A"; multiple scalar args concatenate: chr(65, 97) == "Aa" (documented form).
  • Vector argument: chr([104, 105]) == "hi"; range argument: chr([65:70]) == "ABCDEF" (range expanded, inclusive endpoints per normal range semantics).
  • Code point 0 contributes an empty string; negative values contribute empty. VERIFY: values > 0x10FFFF contribute empty (pin exact upper-bound behavior).
  • verify vs oracle surrogate code points U+D800..U+DFFF — believed to produce nothing/invalid output; pin against oracle since a strict UTF-8 encoder must decide.
  • verify vs oracle non-integer values truncate toward zero (chr(65.7) believed "A"); pin truncation vs rounding.
  • Non-numeric arguments (string, boolean, undef) contribute nothing. VERIFY: whether a WARNING like "chr() parameter could not be converted" is emitted or it is silent.
  • chr() with no arguments returns "". VERIFY zero-arg acceptance without warning.
  • Astral-plane code points work and produce a single character counted once by len: len(chr(128169)) == 1.
  • verify vs oracle nested vectors inside a vector argument (chr([[65]])) — believed to contribute nothing; pin.
  • Roundtrip law for valid inputs: ord(chr(n)) == n; chr(ord(s)) == first character of s.
  • Introduced in 2015.03; in older interpreters an unknown-function WARNING plus undef.
echo(chr(72, 105), chr([33, 33]), chr([97:101]));
