Implementer: Requires a real code-point-to-UTF-8 encoder. Oracle probes: chr(0), chr(-1), chr(55296) (surrogate), chr(1114111), chr(1114112), chr(65.5), chr("x"), chr(). Match silent-vs-warning behavior exactly because --hardwarnings turns any warning into a fatal error.

ord

function 2019.05 
ord(s) -> number

Returns the Unicode code point of the FIRST character of a string.

sA string. Only the first character (first code point) is examined; the rest is ignored.

Decodes the first Unicode code point of the UTF-8 string and returns it as a number. Inverse of chr for single characters. Typically combined with comprehensions to convert whole strings: [for (c = s) ord(c)].

Edge cases & oddities
  • ord("a") == 97; ord("abc") == 97 (first code point only, silently ignores the rest).
  • ord("") -> undef.
  • Non-string argument (number, vector, boolean, undef) -> undef. VERIFY: whether the WARNING "ord() parameter could not be converted" (2019.05-era conversion-warning family) is emitted or the failure is silent.
  • Operates on code points, not grapheme clusters: for a decomposed "é" style string, ord returns 101 (the 'e'), and the combining mark is a separate character reachable via s[1].
  • Astral characters return the full code point (> 0xFFFF), counted as one character: ord of an emoji like U+1F60A returns 128522 — requires true UTF-8 decoding, not UTF-16 surrogate halves.
  • Result is an ordinary double; safe in arithmetic and chr roundtrips.
  • 2019.05+ only: on 2015.03 the call produces an unknown-function WARNING and undef — libraries feature-test with version_num() >= 20190500.
echo(ord("A"), [for (c = "Hi!") ord(c)]);
Implementer: Trivial once strings are stored/decoded as UTF-8 with code-point iteration. Pin the non-string and empty-string warning behavior via oracle. Ensure no surrogate-pair splitting for astral input.

str

function pre-2015.03 
str(value, ...) -> string  (zero or more arguments of any type)

Converts every argument to its display text and concatenates them into one string; the ONLY string concatenation mechanism in the language.

value, ...Any number of values of any type (number, string, boolean, vector, range, undef, function literal). Each is converted to its textual form and appended in order. Zero arguments allowed.

Each argument is rendered using the exact same value formatter that echo uses, with one asymmetry: a string passed DIRECTLY as an argument is inserted unquoted, whereas echo would quote it. Strings nested inside vector arguments ARE quoted, exactly as in echo. Numbers are formatted by the shared 6-significant-digit double-conversion formatter: integers print without a decimal point, trailing zeros are trimmed, fixed notation covers 1e-5 <= |x| < 1e6, and scientific notation with an unpadded exponent is used outside that range. Booleans render as true/false, undef as undef, vectors as [a, b, c] (comma+space, recursive), ranges in spaced-colon form, function literals as a source-like rendering. The results are concatenated with no separators. str never fails and never warns; every value has a rendering.

Edge cases & oddities
  • str() with zero arguments returns "" (empty string).
  • Number formatting is 6 significant digits: str(1/3) == "0.333333", str(PI) == "3.14159", str(10.0) == "10" (no decimal point for integral values), trailing zeros trimmed.
  • Scientific switch at 1e6: str(1000000) == "1e+6", str(1234567) == "1.23457e+6" — large integers go scientific AND GET ROUNDED (famous trap; user code that builds numbers into strings inherits this loss).
  • Small magnitudes: str(0.0001) == "0.0001" AND str(0.00001) == "0.00001" — fixed notation persists down to 1e-5; scientific only below it, unpadded: str(1.1e-6) == "1.1e-6", str(1e100) == "1e+100".
  • str(1/0) == "inf" (after the division-by-zero WARNING from the division itself), str(-1/0) == "-inf", str(0/0) == "nan".
  • Negative zero renders as "0" (UNIQUE_ZERO in the 2021.01 formatter); its sign is observable only via arithmetic (1/-0 == -inf).
  • Top-level string args are unquoted: str("a","b") == "ab" — this is THE concatenation idiom; echo(str(...)) vs echo(...) differ in string quoting.
  • Strings nested inside vector arguments are quoted: str(["a"]) == "[\"a\"]". VERIFY: interior quotes/backslashes in such nested strings are NOT re-escaped in the output (echo("a\"b") style probes).
  • Booleans -> "true"/"false"; undef -> "undef"; empty vector -> "[]"; nested vectors recursive with ", " separators.
  • verify vs oracle ranges render with spaces and explicit step even when implicit: str([0:10]) believed "[0 : 1 : 10]" (pin whether implicit step 1 is displayed).
  • verify vs oracle function-literal arguments render as source-like text, e.g. "function(x, y = 1) x + y" (pin exact spacing/body serialization).
  • str never emits warnings or errors regardless of argument types — total function.
  • Formatting is identical between str, echo, and the assert-message renderer; a single shared formatter is compatibility-critical because golden .echo output and user string-building both depend on it.
  • verify vs oracle dev snapshots have moved toward full-precision shortest-roundtrip number output — 2021.01 baseline must keep 6-significant-digit double-conversion output.
echo(str("r=", 1/3, " v=", [1,"a"], " big=", 1234567));
Implementer: Implement ONE shared value-to-text formatter used by str/echo/assert. Pin via oracle probes: magnitude sweep 10^-7..10^9, negative values, -0, 0.1+0.2, inf/nan, nested vectors with strings containing quotes/backslashes, ranges with and without explicit step, function literals. Deep recursive str() accumulation must not be O(n^2) — use ropes or amortized buffers; libraries build long strings recursively.

string indexing s[i]

operator pre-2015.03 
s[i] -> 1-character string | undef

Zero-based code-point indexing into a string; yields a one-character STRING (there is no char type); anything out of range yields undef.

sThe string being indexed.
iZero-based code-point index; must be a non-negative integer number within [0, len(s)).

s[i] returns the i-th Unicode code point of s as a new string of length 1. Indexing is by code point over the UTF-8 storage, never by byte. Any index that is negative, >= len(s), non-finite, or non-numeric yields undef; a fractional in-range index truncates toward zero. There is no slicing and no negative-from-end indexing; substring extraction is user-space via comprehensions plus str, or recursion.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Result is a string: "abc"[0] == "a", and ("abc"[0])[0] == "a" — chained indexing of the 1-char result works.
  • Out of range (i >= len(s)) -> undef. Negative i -> undef (no Python-style wrapping). VERIFY: both are silent, with no console warning.
  • Fractional indices truncate toward zero: "abc"[1.5] == "b" (same uint32 Trunc conversion as vector indexing); exactly-integral doubles index normally; negative/non-finite/out-of-range/non-numeric yield undef.
  • Index of boolean/string/undef/vector type -> undef. VERIFY: indexing a string with a range or vector yields undef (no fancy multi-index).
  • Code-point correctness: "häx"[1] == "ä"; an astral emoji occupies exactly one index position. Byte-based indexing corrupts any non-ASCII text and is a compatibility bug.
  • Indexing the empty string always yields undef.
  • Grapheme clusters are NOT units: a decomposed accent occupies two indices (base + combining mark).
  • The same postfix [] operator serves vectors; dispatch is by operand type at runtime.
  • undef[0] -> undef. VERIFY: silent vs warning.
s = "héllo"; echo(s[0], s[1], s[10], len(s));
Implementer: Store strings as UTF-8 but index by code point; for performance either cache a code-point offset table per string or accept O(i) scans (real scripts index in loops — memoize). Oracle probes: negative, fractional, huge, and non-numeric indices; multibyte and astral content.

Vector/list builtins

* operator: dot product and matrix multiplication

operator pre-2015.03 
a * b  — shape-dispatched: scalar*scalar | scalar*vector | vector*vector (DOT) | matrix*vector | vector*matrix | matrix*matrix

Multiplication dispatches on operand shape: scalars scale vectors elementwise (recursively), flat-vector*flat-vector is a DOT PRODUCT (scalar result — not elementwise!), and nested row-major lists multiply as matrices.

Dispatch rules: number*number is a product. number*vector (either order) scales elementwise, recursing into nesting, so scalar*matrix works. vector*vector where both are flat numeric vectors of equal length is the dot product, returning a SCALAR. When vectors contain vector elements they are treated as row-major matrices: matrix*vector returns the vector of row dot products (length = row count); vector*matrix returns the vector of column dot products (length = column count, requires len(v) == row count of the matrix); matrix*matrix is the standard product over matching inner dimensions. Any dimension mismatch or non-numeric content yields undef with a WARNING. There is NO elementwise vector*vector multiply anywhere — users write comprehensions. Division: vector/scalar divides elementwise recursively; every other vector division form is undefined.

Edge cases & oddities
  • [1,2,3]*[4,5,6] == 32 — DOT PRODUCT, a scalar; newcomers expecting elementwise [4,10,18] must not get it.
  • Dot with unequal lengths -> undef + WARNING.
  • 2*[1,2] == [2,4]; 2*[[1],[2]] == [[2],[4]] (recursive scaling); [1,2]*2 same.
  • Matrix*vector: [[1,0],[0,2]]*[3,4] == [3,8]; requires every row length == len(v), else undef + warning. Vector*matrix: [3,4]*[[1,0],[0,2]] == [3,8] (column dots); requires len(v) == number of rows.
  • Matrix*matrix: [[1,2]]*[[3],[4]] == [[11]] (1x2 by 2x1); inner-dimension mismatch -> undef + warning.
  • verify vs oracle ragged matrices (rows of differing lengths) — believed whole-result undef + warning, not partial results; pin.
  • verify vs oracle [] * [] and M * [] — believed undef (+ warning); candidate alternative for empty dot is 0; pin against oracle.
  • Non-numeric element anywhere in a dot/matrix operand -> VERIFY whole-result undef vs nan-propagation; strings/bools/undef as operands -> undef + warning.
  • Vectors of vectors of vectors (3+ deep) are not tensors: dispatch only distinguishes 'elements are vectors' vs not — VERIFY exact behavior on depth-3 operands (believed treated as matrices whose 'scalars' are vectors, producing elementwise-recursive adds inside dots — pin, multmatrix-adjacent code hits it).
  • 2/[1,2] is defined: elementwise scalar-by-vector division yields [2, 1]; see the / entry. Only vector/vector division is undef.
  • Dot/matrix results use IEEE double accumulation in element order — VERIFY summation order if bit-exact echo parity of large dots is desired.
  • Same row-major nested-list convention as multmatrix(m); precedence of * over +/- is ordinary.
echo([1,2,3]*[4,5,6], [[1,0],[0,2]]*[3,4], [3,4]*[[1,0],[0,2]], 2*[[1,2],[3,4]], [4,6]/2);
Implementer: Implement dispatch by runtime shape inspection (is-vector of first element), matching OpenSCAD's observable choices; oracle-probe the full mismatch matrix including empty operands, ragged rows, depth-3 nesting, and scalar/vector division forms, capturing warning text. Left-to-right accumulation preserves echo-level determinism.

concat

function 2015.03 
concat(a, b, ...) -> vector  (zero or more arguments)

Single-level list concatenation: list arguments contribute their elements, non-list arguments (INCLUDING strings) are appended as single elements; never deep-flattens.

a, b, ...Any values. A vector argument contributes its top-level elements in order; any non-vector argument (number, string, boolean, range, undef, function) is appended as one element.

Builds a new vector by walking the arguments left to right: for each vector argument, append its elements (one level only — nested vectors stay nested); for each non-vector argument, append the value itself. The result is always a fresh vector; inputs are never modified (nothing in the language mutates).

Edge cases & oddities
  • concat([1], [2,3]) == [1, 2, 3]; single-level only: concat([[1]], [2]) == [[1], 2] — inner vectors are NOT flattened.
  • Non-list args append whole: concat(1, 2) == [1, 2]; concat([1,2], 3) == [1, 2, 3].
  • STRINGS ARE NOT LISTS here: concat("ab", "cd") == ["ab", "cd"] — no character splicing and no string concatenation (a classic wrong expectation; str() concatenates strings).
  • concat() with zero arguments returns []. VERIFY: accepted silently with no warning.
  • undef arguments are kept: concat(undef) == [undef]; concat([], undef, []) == [undef].
  • verify vs oracle a range argument is appended as a single range VALUE, not expanded: concat([1], [2:4]) believed [1, [2 : 1 : 4]] (echo rendering); expansion requires each/comprehension.
  • Booleans and function literals append as single elements.
  • Empty-vector args contribute nothing: concat([], []) == [].
  • 2015.03+: earlier interpreters emit unknown-function WARNING and undef; libraries polyfilled concat with comprehensions.
  • Amortized-O(1) append matters: comprehension+concat chains in recursive library code (pre-'each' style: concat(acc, [x])) are hot paths.
echo(concat([1,2], 3, "ab", [4,[5]], [6:7]));
Implementer: Straightforward; the two behaviors to pin via oracle are zero-arg concat() and range-argument handling. Keep persistent/rope-style vectors or copy-on-append with amortization — recursive concat accumulation is ubiquitous in pre-BOSL2 libraries.

len

function pre-2015.03 
len(value) -> number | undef

Element count of a vector or code-point count of a string; undef for every other type (numbers, booleans, ranges, undef, functions).

valueA vector (returns top-level element count) or a string (returns number of Unicode code points). Any other type yields undef.

For vectors, returns the number of top-level elements (nested vectors count as one element each). For strings, returns the number of Unicode code points (not bytes; astral characters count 1). For any other type — number, boolean, undef, range, function literal — the result is undef. Ranges deliberately have no len: they are a distinct non-list type.

Edge cases & oddities
  • len([1,2,3]) == 3; len([]) == 0; len([[1,2],[3]]) == 2 (top level only).
  • len("häx") == 3 (code points, not bytes); len("") == 0; a single astral emoji has len 1.
  • len(5), len(true), len(undef), len(function(x) x) -> undef. VERIFY: whether 2021.01 emits a WARNING of the "len() parameter could not be converted" conversion-failure family or returns undef silently — pin exact text; --hardwarnings users depend on the set of warnings.
  • len([0:10]) -> undef — ranges are not lists (is_list false). begin/step/end ARE extractable in 2021.01 (r.begin/r.step/r.end, or r[0]/r[1]/r[2]), so the element count is computable as floor((end-begin)/step)+1 for a non-empty range.
  • len is the standard guard in recursive list processing (i >= len(v) base case); it must be O(1).
  • len of the result of search/concat/comprehensions behaves as for any vector.
  • No arguments or multiple arguments: VERIFY error/warning behavior (believed too-many/too-few argument warning with undef).
echo(len([1,2,3]), len("héllo"), len(5), len([0:3]));
Implementer: O(1) for vectors; for strings either store code-point count eagerly or cache on first query. Oracle probes: len on every type including ranges and function values, plus the warning text question.

lookup

function pre-2015.03 
lookup(key, table) -> number  where table = [[k0,v0],[k1,v1],...] with numeric keys in ascending order

Piecewise-LINEAR interpolation of values over a numeric key table, clamped to the first/last value outside the key range.

keyNumeric lookup key.
tableVector of [key, value] pairs; keys and values numeric; keys expected in ascending order.

If key exactly equals a table key, the stored value is returned exactly. If key falls between two neighboring keys k_i < key < k_j, the result is linear interpolation v_i + (v_j - v_i) * (key - k_i) / (k_j - k_i). If key is below the smallest key, the FIRST value is returned; above the largest key, the LAST value — i.e. the function clamps, it never extrapolates. Canonical uses: gear/thread tables, animation easing over $t.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Exact hit returns the stored value with no interpolation arithmetic (bit-exact): lookup(0.5, [[0,0],[0.5,1],[1,0]]) == 1.
  • Interpolation: lookup(0.25, [[0,0],[0.5,1],[1,0]]) == 0.5.
  • Clamping: lookup(-99, t) == first value; lookup(99, t) == last value; key = +/-inf clamps likewise (VERIFY inf handling).
  • Single-pair table -> that value for every key.
  • Empty table -> undef. VERIFY: accompanying WARNING text (a lookup-specific message exists in the conversion-warning family; pin wording).
  • verify vs oracle duplicate/equal adjacent keys — interpolating between them divides by zero; pin whether the result is the stored value (exact-hit path wins), nan, or inf.
  • verify vs oracle unsorted tables — the documented contract requires ascending keys; the actual algorithm (believed nearest-bracketing scan) may still produce SOME deterministic answer on unsorted input; pin the exact behavior rather than asserting, because sloppy real-world tables exist.
  • verify vs oracle rows with extra columns ([k, v, junk]) — believed extra entries ignored; rows that are scalars or wrong-typed — believed warning + row skipped or undef result; pin.
  • Non-numeric key (string/undef/vector) -> undef (VERIFY warning); key = nan -> VERIFY (comparisons with nan all fail; candidate outcomes: first value, last value, or nan — pin exactly).
  • Values must be scalars in 2021.01 — no vector-valued interpolation (feature exists only as later discussion/snapshot work); vector values -> VERIFY undef/warning.
  • Table itself non-vector -> undef + VERIFY warning.
t = [[0,0],[50,20],[100,25]]; echo(lookup(0,t), lookup(75,t), lookup(200,t));
Implementer: Pin the degenerate-table behaviors (empty, duplicate keys, unsorted, nan key) via oracle probes before choosing an implementation — a binary search implementation and a linear bracketing scan agree on well-formed input but can diverge on unsorted tables, and real scripts feed it garbage.

search

function pre-2015.03 
search(match_value, string_or_vector, num_returns_per_match = 1, index_col_num = 0) -> vector (shape depends on num_returns_per_match)

Per-term index lookup in a string, flat vector, or table — with per-CHARACTER expansion of string search terms and mode-dependent ragged result shapes that must be cloned bug-for-bug.

match_valueThe search term(s). A STRING is split into one term PER CHARACTER (it is NOT substring search). A vector supplies one term per element (elements may be strings, numbers, or other values). A bare scalar acts as a single term (VERIFY acceptance).
string_or_vectorThe target: a string (terms compared against each character), a flat vector (terms compared against each element), or a table — a vector of row-vectors — where terms are compared against row[index_col_num].
num_returns_per_matchDefault 1: return only the FIRST match index per term, as a BARE index in a flat result. 0: return ALL match indices per term, as one sub-vector per term. N>1: up to N match indices per term, as one sub-vector per term.
index_col_numDefault 0. When target rows are vectors, the column within each row that terms are compared against. Ignored for string targets and plain scalar elements (VERIFY).

search runs an independent scan of the target for each search term and combines per-term results into one output vector, in term order. Matching uses the language's == equality (so type-mismatched comparisons simply never match). Result shape is mode-dependent and deliberately irregular: with num_returns_per_match=1 the output is a FLAT vector of bare indices; with 0 or N>1 the output is a vector of sub-vectors, one per term, each holding that term's match indices in ascending target order (empty sub-vector [] for a term with no match). Because a string match_value explodes into per-character terms, search("abc", s) finds the characters a, b, c separately — to search for a whole string as an element of a list you must wrap it: search(["abc"], list).

Edge cases & oddities
  • Per-character terms: search("a","abcdabcd") == [0]; search("ab","abcdabcd") == [0,1]; search("ba","abcdabcd") == [1,0] (result order follows TERM order, not target order); search("aa","aa") == [0,0] (duplicate terms searched independently).
  • All-matches mode: search("a","abcdabcd",0) == [[0,4]]; search("ab","abcdabcd",0) == [[0,4],[1,5]].
  • No-match, default mode: search("e","abcdabcd") == [] — the documented example. VERIFY (critical, two candidate behaviors): with num_returns_per_match=1, an unmatched term is believed to be SKIPPED ENTIRELY from the flat output (so the result can be SHORTER than the term list and indices cannot be aligned back to terms — the documented reason to use mode 0), accompanied by a console message approximately 'WARNING: search term not found: "e"'; the alternative candidate is that the term contributes an [] element producing mixed shapes like [0, [], 2]. Pin against the oracle and clone exactly — libraries pattern-match this.
  • No-match in modes 0 / N>1: the term's slot holds [] — search("abe","abcd",0) == [[0],[1],[]] — ragged shapes are normal and load-bearing.
  • verify vs oracle the not-found console message — exact wording, whether it appears in all modes or only default mode, and once per missing term.
  • Whole-string element search requires wrapping: search(["b"], ["a","b","c"]) == [1]; bare search("b", ["a","b","c"]) also finds index 1 but ONLY because the term is a single character comparing equal to the 1-char string elements; a multi-character term (from a wrapped list) compares whole-element.
  • Table search: search("b", [["a",1],["b",2],["b",3]], 0) == [[1,2]] (comparing column 0); with index_col_num=1: search(2, [["a",1],["b",2]], 0, 1) == [[1]] (VERIFY scalar match_value acceptance here; wrap as [2] if the oracle warns).
  • verify vs oracle scalar (number/boolean) match_value as a single term on a flat vector: search(3, [5,3,3]) believed [1]; pin whether a warning demands a list.
  • num_returns_per_match=N>1 returns AT MOST N indices, fewer if fewer matches exist: search("a","aaaa",3) == [[0,1,2]]... VERIFY exact — believed [[0,1,2]].
  • verify vs oracle negative or fractional num_returns_per_match — believed truncated to int; pin whether negative behaves like 0 (all) or errors.
  • Empty match_value ("" or []) -> []; empty target -> every term unmatched (mode-dependent shape).
  • Type-strict matching: number 1 never matches string "1"; undef terms — VERIFY whether undef==undef equality lets an undef term match undef elements.
  • verify vs oracle rows shorter than index_col_num, or scalar rows in a table search — believed console warning (approx 'search: invalid table') with the row skipped; pin text and whether the term can still match later valid rows.
  • verify vs oracle vector-valued terms matching vector elements/columns via deep equality — believed to work (== is deep); pin.
  • verify vs oracle index_col_num with a string target — believed ignored/invalid; pin.
  • Matching is always by equality — no wildcards, no regex, no substring, no case folding, anywhere.
echo(search("ab","abcdabcd"), search("abe","abcd",0), search(["b",2], [["a",1],["b",2]], 0));
Implementer: This is the most quirk-dense builtin; write an exhaustive oracle probe matrix over {string/list/scalar match_value} x {string/flat/table target} x {num_returns 1,0,2} x {hit, miss, multi-hit} x {invalid rows} and freeze every output shape and console message verbatim BEFORE implementing. Do not normalize the ragged shapes or 'fix' the mode-1 miss behavior — BOSL2-era and Thingiverse code depends on the exact quirks.

vector addition and subtraction

operator pre-2015.03 
v1 + v2, v1 - v2, -v  (equal-length vectors, recursive elementwise); vector/scalar mixes -> undef

Elementwise + and - on EQUAL-length vectors (recursing into nested vectors); NO scalar broadcasting — scalar+vector and length mismatches yield undef with a warning.

When both operands are vectors of the same length, + and - operate elementwise, recursing into nested vectors (so matrices add naturally). Unary minus negates elementwise recursively. There is NO broadcasting: a scalar added to a vector, or two vectors of different lengths, produce undef and a console WARNING, and evaluation continues. Elementwise recursion applies the same rules at each level, so mismatches deep inside a structure surface as undef at that position or for that sub-operation.

Edge cases & oddities
  • [1,2] + [10,20] == [11,22]; nested: [1,[2,3]] + [10,[20,30]] == [11,[22,33]]; [] + [] == [].
  • Length mismatch: [1,2] + [1,2,3] -> undef with WARNING (approx 'undefined operation ([1, 2] + [1, 2, 3])' — VERIFY exact operand rendering). VERIFY: ancient pre-2015 releases truncated to the shorter length; 2021.01 must be undef+warn.
  • NO broadcasting: [1,2] + 1 -> undef + WARNING; 1 + [1,2] likewise; users write [for (x=v) x+1].
  • verify vs oracle element-level type mismatch inside equal-length vectors ([1,"a"] + [1,2]) — believed to produce undef AT THAT ELEMENT ([2, undef]) with a warning, not a whole-result undef; pin, libraries observe both the shape and the warning count.
  • Unary minus: -[1,[2,-3]] == [-1,[-2,3]]; -undef -> undef; -"a" -> undef + warning.
  • Ranges and strings as operands -> undef + warning (ranges do not behave as vectors in arithmetic).
  • Booleans as elements or operands -> undef (that element/operation) + warning.
  • undef operand -> undef (VERIFY whether an additional warning fires or the earlier undef's warning suffices).
  • nan/inf elements flow through elementwise arithmetic by IEEE rules with no extra warnings.
  • Every warning counts under --hardwarnings; the warning SET is compatibility surface.
echo([1,2]+[3,4], [1,2]-[3,4,5], [1,2]+1, -[1,[2,3]]);
Implementer: Oracle-probe the mismatch matrix (scalar+vec, len mismatch, nested mismatch, string/bool/range/undef elements) recording BOTH the value shape and the exact warning lines/counts. Do not add broadcasting as an extension default — it changes undef-propagation behavior real scripts test for.

Other builtins: echo, assert, version, introspection

assert

module 2019.05 
assert(condition);  assert(condition, message);   |   assert(condition, message) expression   (expression form 2019.05+)

Halts evaluation with a formatted ERROR plus TRACE stack when the condition is falsy; the expression form otherwise yields its trailing expression. The only throwing construct in the language.

conditionAny value, tested with standard truthiness: exactly the five falsy values — false, 0 (and -0), "", [], undef — FAIL; everything else passes, including nan (assert(0/0) passes), "false", [0], and -1.
messageOptional; any value, rendered with the echo value formatter and appended to the error line after ': '. VERIFY: evaluated eagerly with the call (believed) rather than only on failure.

On a truthy condition, the statement form does nothing (its children, if any, instantiate normally — VERIFY) and the expression form evaluates to its trailing expression. On a falsy condition, evaluation HALTS immediately with an ERROR line naming the re-serialized SOURCE TEXT of the condition expression, the optional message, and the file and line, followed by one 'TRACE:' line per frame of the module instantiation stack. Nothing renders; the CLI exits nonzero. Expression asserts chain: assert(a) assert(b) expr. Introduced 2019.05; the canonical argument-validation tool for libraries.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Failure output shape: ERROR: Assertion '(cond-source-text)' failed: "message" in file <path>, line <N> — then TRACE: called by '<module>', in file <path>, line <N> lines unwinding the module stack innermost-first. VERIFY every punctuation detail verbatim (quoting of the condition, parentheses, message quoting, comma placement) — users and CI parse it.
  • The condition text is the AST re-serialization, not verbatim source: normalized whitespace and possibly added parentheses (assert(1+1==3) may render as '((1 + 1) == 3)') — VERIFY exact serializer output on a probe battery.
  • Message rendered like an echo VALUE: string messages believed printed WITH quotes; non-string messages (numbers, vectors) render in display form; omitted message omits the ': msg' segment entirely.
  • Truthiness traps: assert([]) FAILS (empty vector falsy); assert("false") PASSES; assert(0/0) PASSES (nan is truthy — toBool is a plain != 0 test); assert(undef) fails.
  • Expression form: assert(cond, msg) expr — lazy only in the sense that expr is unreached on failure; chains and mixes with let/echo expressions; inside functions a failure reports the assert's own file/line with the MODULE instantiation TRACE (VERIFY whether intermediate function call sites appear — believed not, modules only).
  • verify vs oracle statement-form assert with a child (assert(x) cube();) — believed the child instantiates normally on success; pin.
  • verify vs oracle assert() with no arguments and assert(a, b, extra) — pin the too-few/too-many argument warnings/errors.
  • Halting is total: no further echoes, warnings, or geometry from anywhere in the tree after the failure (VERIFY whether top-level statements after the failing one are reached — believed not).
  • Pre-2019.05 compatibility: on 2015.03 assert is an unknown module (warned, ignored) / unknown function — version-gate via version_num.
  • Not affected by --hardwarnings (already fatal); assert is the ONLY user-reachable hard stop besides syntax/recursion errors.
module ring(r) { assert(r > 0, "radius must be positive"); circle(r); } ring(5);
Implementer: Implement an AST-to-source serializer for the condition text and freeze it against oracle probes (operators, calls, indexing, ternaries). Halt via an evaluator-level exception that still flushes prior console output in order. Exit code and stderr formatting are part of the CLI contract.

echo

module pre-2015.03 (module form); 2019.05 (expression form) 
echo(arg, ..., name=value, ...);   |   echo(args...) expression   (expression form 2019.05+)

Prints one console line 'ECHO: ...' at instantiation/evaluation time; the expression form additionally evaluates to its trailing expression, enabling tracing inside functions.

arg, ...Positional values of any type, printed in order joined by ', ' using the shared value formatter (strings quoted, numbers 6-sig-digit %g, vectors recursive).
name=value, ...Named arguments print as 'name = value', interleaved with positionals in written order. $-prefixed named arguments are NOT printed — they set special variables instead (VERIFY).

Module/statement form: echo(...) emits exactly one line, 'ECHO: ' followed by the rendered arguments joined by ', '. Emission happens during tree instantiation, depth-first, in source order — before and independent of geometry evaluation, and NOT suppressed by geometry caching (a module instantiated 100 times echoes 100 times). Expression form (2019.05): 'echo(args) expr' prints the line, then evaluates and yields expr; it prints on EVERY evaluation, making it the only tracing tool inside functions and comprehensions; echo-expressions chain and nest with let/assert expressions.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Rendering: strings quoted at top level (echo("hi") -> ECHO: "hi") — the deliberate asymmetry with str(); numbers exactly as str() (6 significant digits, 1234567 -> 1.23457e+6, inf/nan); vectors [a, b, c] with interior strings quoted; booleans true/false; undef prints undef; ranges spaced-colon form (VERIFY implicit step display); function literals as source-like text (VERIFY exact).
  • verify vs oracle interior quotes/backslashes in printed strings are NOT re-escaped (echo("a\"b") believed 'ECHO: "a"b"').
  • Named args: echo(b=2) -> 'ECHO: b = 2' (spaces around =); mixed echo(1, x=2, 3) preserves written order (VERIFY ordering).
  • verify vs oracle echo() with no arguments prints bare 'ECHO: ' (trailing space presence — pin exactly for golden tests).
  • verify vs oracle $-named args are consumed as special-variable settings, not printed: echo($fn=5) believed to print 'ECHO: ' with nothing (pin).
  • Expression form with no trailing expression is a parse error; the form binds tightly: x = echo("trace") compute(y); is the idiom; prints once per EVALUATION, so inside a recursive function it prints per call — recursion tracing works.
  • verify vs oracle children given to the module form (echo("x") cube();) — believed accepted and IGNORED silently in 2021.01 (pin; some snapshots warn that echo does not support children).
  • Timing/order: echoes appear in depth-first instantiation order, interleaved with WARNINGs; all echoes precede rendering output; re-compiles (F5 then F6, or each animation frame) re-emit everything; CLI writes ECHO lines to the console (VERIFY stderr-vs-stdout) or to a file with -o out.echo — the .echo export is the golden-test oracle format.
  • Rate limiting of duplicate lines is GUI console behavior only, not language semantics — the CLI/.echo stream contains every line.
  • No return value in module form; in expression form the echo itself has no value — only the trailing expression's value passes through.
echo("v=", [1,"a"], x=42); function f(n) = echo("n is", n) n*2; y = f(3);
Implementer: Route all output through the shared str/echo formatter. Support -o file.echo equivalent early — it is the single most useful differential-testing channel against the official binary. Pin: zero-arg output, $-arg swallowing, child handling, stream (stderr), and quoting of embedded quotes.

parent_module

function 2014.03 
parent_module(n) -> string  (module name n levels up the instantiation stack)

Introspects the dynamic module instantiation stack by name; with $parent_modules it enables the classic echo-driven BOM pattern.

nStack depth to look up: 0 is the currently executing module, 1 its instantiating parent, and so on. Valid range is [0, $parent_modules - 1].

Returns the NAME (a string) of the module n levels above the current point in the dynamic instantiation stack — the stack of module calls that led to the current instantiation, not the lexical nesting. The canonical use is bill-of-materials generation: a part module echoes str("part used by: ", parent_module(1)), or walks the whole stack with for (i = [0 : $parent_modules - 1]).

Edge cases & oddities
  • verify vs oracle parent_module(0) returns the CURRENT module's own name (believed; pin the zero-point convention — off-by-one here silently breaks BOM scripts).
  • Out-of-range n (>= $parent_modules, or any call at top level where the stack is empty): believed an ERROR approximately 'Parent module index (n) out of range' — VERIFY exact text and whether it hard-stops or warns and yields undef.
  • verify vs oracle negative or fractional n — pin truncation vs error.
  • The stack is DYNAMIC: the same module reports different parents depending on call path; for-loops, if-blocks and anonymous braces do not push named frames.
  • verify vs oracle whether built-in module instantiations (translate, union, ...) appear as frames or only user-defined modules — pin, it changes every reported depth/name.
  • Functions do not push module frames: calling parent_module inside a function reflects the module context the function was called from (VERIFY).
  • children() re-instantiation: children evaluate with the instantiating site's dynamic context — VERIFY which stack a child's parent_module sees (believed the stack at the children() call, i.e. including the callee module).
  • Interacts with the geometry cache: echo-based BOM output re-emits per instantiation regardless of caching (echoes are not cached away).
  • Returns a plain string comparable with == for dispatch-on-caller tricks (real scripts do this).
module part() { echo(str("part called by: ", parent_module(1))); cube(1); } module asm() part(); asm();
Implementer: Maintain an explicit instantiation-stack of module names in the evaluator (needed anyway for TRACE output and recursion detection). Oracle-pin: the index-0 convention, out-of-range message, and whether builtin modules count as frames — the three highest-risk details.

$parent_modules

$ var 2014.03 
$parent_modules -> number  (current module instantiation stack depth)

The depth of the module instantiation stack at the point of evaluation; the loop bound for parent_module(n).

A dynamically scoped special variable holding how many module frames are on the instantiation stack. At the top level it is 0; inside a user module called from the top level it is 1 (VERIFY the exact counting origin together with parent_module's index-0 convention — the two must stay consistent: valid parent_module indices are [0, $parent_modules - 1]).

Edge cases & oddities
  • verify vs oracle top-level value is 0 and each user-module instantiation increments by 1; pin whether builtin modules increment (must match parent_module's frame rule).
  • Like all $-variables it is readable anywhere, including inside functions, reflecting the dynamic call context.
  • verify vs oracle assigning to it ($parent_modules = 5;) — believed a legal but meaningless shadow that the engine's own reads ignore or that actually shadows for descendants; pin (same question applies to $children).
  • Canonical idiom: for (i = [0 : $parent_modules - 1]) echo(parent_module(i)); — note that at depth 0 this range is [0 : -1], which under 2021.01's legacy range-swap rules DEPRECATION-warns and iterates — real BOM scripts guard with if ($parent_modules > 0).
module a() b(); module b() echo(depth = $parent_modules); a();
Implementer: Derive from the same evaluator stack as parent_module and TRACE; never store independently. Pin counting origin via oracle before freezing.

rands

function pre-2015.03 (RNG algorithm changed across early versions — VERIFY which release fixed the current mt19937 behavior) 
rands(min_value, max_value, value_count, seed_value = undef) -> vector of numbers

Vector of uniformly distributed random doubles; deterministic per call when seeded; ALWAYS returns a vector, even for a single value.

min_valueLower bound of the uniform range.
max_valueUpper bound of the uniform range.
value_countHow many values to generate. 0 yields [].
seed_valueOptional numeric seed. When given, the call uses a FRESH generator seeded with it, so identical seeded calls return identical vectors. When omitted, values come from a shared nondeterministic stream.

Returns a vector of value_count doubles uniformly distributed over [min_value, max_value]. With a seed, results are reproducible: the same (min, max, count, seed) always yields the same vector within a given OpenSCAD build, and two identical seeded calls in one script are identical to each other (each seeded call re-seeds a fresh generator). Without a seed, each call draws from a global stream seeded unpredictably, differing between runs and between preview/render compiles.

Edge cases & oddities
  • ALWAYS a vector: rands(0,1,1) == [x] (single-element vector — scripts index [0]); rands(0,1,0) == [].
  • verify vs oracle negative value_count — believed [] plus a WARNING (approx 'rands() cannot create a negative number of random values'); fractional counts believed truncated toward zero; pin both.
  • min_value == max_value -> every element exactly that value.
  • verify vs oracle min > max — believed the bounds are swapped (or C++-UB territory in the official build); pin actual output and whether a warning fires.
  • verify vs oracle endpoint inclusivity — documented as inclusive [min, max], but std::uniform_real_distribution is [min, max); observable only statistically, document as [min, max) reachable-in-practice.
  • undef/nan/inf bounds or count: VERIFY — believed undef result or nan-filled vector with conversion warning; pin each combination.
  • Seed handling: VERIFY fractional seeds (4 vs 4.5 — believed distinct via internal conversion, but pin truncation/collision behavior), negative seeds, and seed 0 vs unseeded distinction.
  • Seeded reproducibility holds within one OpenSCAD version/build only: the PRNG is std::mt19937 fed through the C++ STANDARD-LIBRARY-SPECIFIC uniform_real_distribution — official Windows/Mac/Linux builds may already differ from each other (VERIFY), and pre-2015 versions used a different RNG entirely.
  • Unseeded randomness re-rolls on every compile: preview then render produce different geometry — a known user gotcha; seeded calls are stable across F5/F6.
  • rands drives no geometry caching exemption: two instantiations of a module containing unseeded rands produce different values (module bodies re-evaluate; only geometry results are cached — VERIFY the interaction: identical subtrees with unseeded rands and whether cache collapses them).
echo(rands(0, 10, 3, 42), rands(0, 10, 3, 42), rands(5, 5, 2));
Implementer: Promise seeded self-determinism (same seed -> same vector in YOUR implementation), and explicitly DO NOT promise bit-compatibility with OpenSCAD's sequences — that would require cloning std::mt19937 plus a specific stdlib's uniform_real_distribution, and the official builds may not even agree cross-platform. Oracle probes: negative/fractional counts, swapped bounds, fractional seeds, warning texts.

version

function pre-2015.03 
version() -> [year, month] (release builds) | [year, month, day] (date-versioned snapshots)

Returns the OpenSCAD version as a numeric vector, e.g. [2021, 1]; the vector-shaped twin of version_num().

Returns a vector of numbers identifying the running interpreter (two elements on release builds, three on date-versioned snapshot builds): [2021, 1] for release 2021.01. Libraries use it (usually via version_num, the portable spelling: vector ordering only became defined in 2021.01 — earlier interpreters silently evaluated any vector comparison to FALSE) to feature-gate code paths.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Release mapping: 2021.01 -> [2021, 1]; 2019.05 -> [2019, 5]; 2015.03 -> [2015, 3]. VERIFY: point releases like 2015.03-3 -> believed [2015, 3, 3].
  • Dev snapshots report their snapshot date: believed [YYYY, M, D] (e.g. a 2025-02-19 nightly -> [2025, 2, 19]) — VERIFY mapping.
  • In 2021.01 vector ordering IS defined, so version() > [2019,5,0] evaluates to true. On pre-2021 interpreters the same comparison silently evaluated to FALSE (never undef) — a worse trap, since it silently disables the feature gate — which is why version_num() remains the conventional, portable spelling. A 2021.01-conformant reimplementation must implement vector comparison.
  • verify vs oracle arguments passed to version() — believed ignored, possibly with an unused-argument warning; pin.
  • What a reimplementation reports is a policy decision with real consequences: report [2021, 1] to unlock 2021.01 codepaths in BOSL2 etc.; reporting a fake future date may enable snapshot-only paths that then fail.
  • Release versions are two-element vectors: version() == [2021, 1] on the 2021.01 release, so version()[2] is undef and len(version()) == 2. A third (day) element appears only on date-versioned snapshot builds. version_num() is unaffected: the missing day fills as 0, giving 20210100.
echo(version(), version()[0] >= 2021);
Implementer: Report [2021, 1] for a 2021.01-compat mode; if the app exposes opt-in snapshot features, still consider keeping version() at the compat baseline and documenting the choice — libraries key behavior off it.

version_num

function pre-2015.03 
version_num() -> number  (YYYYMMPP)

Returns the version as a single comparable number, e.g. 20210100 for release 2021.01 — the standard feature-test value.

Encodes the version as YYYYMMPP: year * 10000 + month * 100 + patch. Release 2021.01 returns 20210100. Because it is a plain number it works with relational operators, making it the idiomatic feature gate: if (version_num() >= 20190500) use 2019.05 features.

Edge cases & oddities
  • 2021.01 -> 20210100; 2019.05 -> 20190500; 2015.03 -> 20150300. VERIFY: patch releases (2015.03-2 believed 20150302).
  • Dev snapshots: believed YYYYMMDD of the snapshot date (e.g. 20250219) — which makes snapshot values numerically interleave oddly with release+patch encoding; VERIFY mapping before mimicking snapshots.
  • Returns a double like every OpenSCAD number; 6-significant-digit echo formatting renders it in scientific notation (echo(version_num()) -> 2.02101e+7) — the VALUE is exact even though the echo text is rounded; feature tests compare the value, not the text.
  • verify vs oracle arguments ignored / warned.
  • Real-world gates to satisfy: >= 20150300 (concat/chr/text era), >= 20190500 (assert/ord/each era), >= 20210100 (function literals/^). Whatever you report toggles those library branches.
echo(version_num()); if (version_num() >= 20210100) echo("function literals available");
Implementer: Keep version() and version_num() consistent with each other. Report 20210100 in compat mode. Document that echo's scientific rendering of the value is expected and correct.

Special variables ($-variables)

$children

$ var pre-2015.03 (predates 2014.03 — existed with legacy child(); 2014.03 fixed a dynamic-scoping bug and added children()/parent_module()/$parent_modules) 
$children  // integer, set by the module-call mechanism

Number of child instantiations passed to the currently executing module; 0 at top level and for childless calls.

Automatically bound on every module invocation to the count of children at the call site, before the body runs. Used to guard children(i) indexing and to loop 'for (i = [0:1:$children-1]) children(i);'. Dynamically scoped like all specials: nested module calls each rebind it; function calls made from the body see the module's value.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Top level: $children is 0 (the implicit root group has no 'passed' children in the user-visible sense). VERIFY: 0 vs unknown-variable at top level — pin via oracle.
  • A single child without braces counts as 1; a braced block 'm(){a();b();}' counts each statement: 2; an empty block {} gives 0.
  • A for-loop as a child counts as ONE child (the loop is a single group node), not one per iteration — classic trap; children([0:$children-1]) over a for-child sees 1.
  • if/else as a child likewise counts as 1 regardless of which branch is taken (VERIFY: including a false-with-no-else if, which yields an empty group child).
  • Assigning $children yourself is legal syntax (any $-var is assignable) but is immediately shadowed at the next module call; scripts doing this get self-inflicted confusion, not an error.
  • children(i) with i outside 0..$children-1 warns/errors (message owned by children(); see modules category).
  • $children inside a function called from the module body resolves dynamically to the calling module's count.
  • Range guard idiom 'if ($children > 1)' is common in libraries (e.g. conditional union wrappers) — must be exact.
module pair() { children(0); translate([20, 0, 0]) children($children - 1); }
pair() { cube(5); sphere(3); } // echo($children) inside would print 2
Implementer: Bind it in the module-call frame alongside the children list; ensure for/if children count as single group nodes. Oracle-probe top-level read and the for-as-one-child case.

$fa

$ var pre-2015.03 
$fa = <number>  // default 12 (degrees)

Minimum angle per circle fragment in degrees; caps 360/$fa fragments. Used only when $fn == 0.

valueDegrees per fragment. Values below 0.01 are clamped to 0.01 with a WARNING.

One of the two adaptive tessellation knobs. When $fn==0, fragments = ceil(max(min(360/$fa, 2*PI*r/$fs), 5)): $fa contributes the angular cap 360/$fa, $fs contributes the arc-length cap, and whichever demands FEWER fragments wins the min(), with an absolute floor of 5 fragments. Dynamically scoped; settable per call or via -D.

Edge cases & oddities
  • $fa < 0.01 (including 0 and negatives) is clamped to 0.01 and emits a WARNING approximately '$fa too small - clamping to 0.01'. VERIFY: exact message text and whether the warning fires at assignment time or at first use.
  • Default 12 gives 360/12 = 30 fragments for large radii (when $fs is not the binding constraint).
  • Ignored entirely whenever $fn > 0 in scope — no warning about the shadowed value.
  • The min() with the $fs term means lowering $fa alone does NOT increase fragments if $fs already binds (common confusion; both must be lowered for small parts).
  • Absolute minimum of 5 fragments applies on this path (contrast: the $fn path floors at 3).
  • Radius below GRID_FINE (2^-20) short-circuits to 3 fragments before $fa is consulted.
  • Non-numeric $fa: VERIFY behavior (expected: type error/warning and fallback to default) — pin via oracle.
  • Consumers identical to $fn: circle, sphere, cylinder(max r), rotate_extrude (angle-scaled), offset(r), text, linear_extrude twist slices default.
$fa = 1; $fs = 0.4; // high-quality print settings idiom
sphere(r = 10);
Implementer: Clamp-warning text and trigger point are observable surface (users run --hardwarnings). Probe with $fa=0.001 and capture the exact console line. The min/max/ceil composition must match bit-for-bit at boundary radii; test radii where 360/$fa == 2*PI*r/$fs exactly.

$fn

$ var pre-2015.03 
$fn = <number>  // default 0

Fragment-count override for circle tessellation: when > 0 it forces the exact number of fragments for a full circle, bypassing $fa/$fs entirely.

valueNumber of fragments for a full 360-degree circle. 0 (default) means 'use $fa/$fs formula'. Values 1 and 2 are raised to the minimum of 3.

Dynamically scoped like all $-variables. Read by every consumer of the fragment formula: circle(), sphere() (meridian count; latitude rings = ceil(N/2)), cylinder() (fragments from max(r1,r2)), rotate_extrude() (scaled by angle/360), offset(r=...), text() glyph-outline flattening, and the default slices computation of linear_extrude() with twist. When $fn > 0 the fragment count is max(int($fn), 3) and $fa/$fs are ignored completely. Can be set at any scope level, per-call as a named argument (sphere(10, $fn=64)), via let($fn=...), or injected with -D '$fn=64'.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Full fragment formula (documented in the manual): if r < GRID_FINE (2^-20 ~= 9.5367e-7) -> 3 fragments regardless of everything else; else if $fn > 0 -> max($fn,3) as integer; else -> ceil(max(min(360/$fa, 2*PI*r/$fs), 5)).
  • verify vs oracle fractional $fn is truncated toward zero when cast to int (e.g. $fn=4.9 -> 4 fragments), not rounded or ceiled — pin via oracle.
  • $fn=1 and $fn=2 silently become 3 fragments; no warning is emitted.
  • Negative $fn fails the '> 0' test and falls through to the $fa/$fs path (it is NOT clamped to 3). NaN or +/-inf $fn instead short-circuits to exactly 3 fragments (it shares the tiny-radius branch, tested BEFORE $fn > 0).
  • $fn=0 is the documented way to re-enable $fa/$fs after a coarser scope set $fn.
  • Very large $fn (e.g. 1000) emits no warning; it just produces huge meshes. Performance behavior is user-visible.
  • $fn set as a call argument affects the whole subtree of that call (dynamic scoping), including children() re-instantiations under a module.
  • sphere($fn=6) yields 6 meridians and ceil(6/2)=3 latitude rings at half-step phase offsets — no pole vertices; models depend on this exact shape.
  • circle first vertex at angle 0 on +X, CCW winding, polygon INSCRIBED — $fn=6 hex-nut idiom and r/cos(180/n) compensation must keep working.
  • $fn is not consumed by cube/square/polygon/polyhedron; passing it there is harmless (all $-args are always accepted).
  • verify vs oracle rotate_extrude with $fn and partial angle uses max(ceil($fn * angle/360), 1) fragments for the sweep — pin exact rounding.
  • verify vs oracle text() maps $fn to bezier-flattening segment count of glyph outlines; exact mapping (segments per full turn vs per curve) must be pinned via oracle.
$fn = 6;
cylinder(h = 3, r = 5.5 / cos(180 / 6)); // circumscribed hex for an M10 nut
echo($fn); // ECHO: 6
Implementer: Pin the exact formula (including the GRID_FINE=2^-20 tiny-radius short-circuit, int truncation of $fn, and the floor of 3) with oracle probes: echo-free geometry probes via .csg/STL vertex counts at $fn in {0,1,2,2.5,4.9,6}. Fragment count feeds mesh topology, so a one-off here breaks every golden mesh test.

$fs

$ var pre-2015.03 
$fs = <number>  // default 2 (units)

Minimum fragment size (arc length in model units); caps 2*PI*r/$fs fragments. Used only when $fn == 0.

valueMinimum arc length per fragment. Values below 0.01 are clamped to 0.01 with a WARNING.

Partner knob to $fa in the adaptive formula ceil(max(min(360/$fa, 2*PI*r/$fs), 5)). For small radii the $fs term is small, the min() picks it, and the floor of 5 fragments takes over — this is why tiny circles render as pentagons by default. Dynamically scoped; settable per call or via -D.

Edge cases & oddities
  • $fs < 0.01 clamped to 0.01 with WARNING approximately '$fs too small - clamping to 0.01'. VERIFY exact text and trigger point.
  • Default $fs=2 makes a default circle(r=1) hit the 5-fragment floor (2*PI*1/2 ~= 3.14 < 5): a default tiny circle is a pentagon — widely observed behavior, must reproduce.
  • Ignored when $fn > 0.
  • The formula uses arc length (2*PI*r/$fs), not chord length — do not 'improve' to chords.
  • For cylinder the r in the formula is max(r1,r2); a cone with r1=10,r2=0 tessellates like r=10.
  • r < GRID_FINE short-circuits to 3 fragments before $fs is consulted.
  • verify vs oracle offset(r=x) feeds |x| as the radius into the formula for corner-rounding fragments — pin via oracle.
  • Warning set (clamped $fa/$fs) counts toward --hardwarnings fatality.
$fs = 0.5;
circle(r = 1); // more than the default 5 fragments now
// with defaults ($fa=12,$fs=2) circle(r=1) is a pentagon
Implementer: Golden-test the 5-fragment floor with circle(r=1) defaults and the crossover radius where $fa starts binding (r where 2*PI*r/$fs > 360/$fa, i.e. r > 30*2/(2*PI) ~= 9.549 at defaults). Vertex counts in exported STL/CSG are the oracle.

$preview

$ var 2019.05 
$preview  // boolean, set by the evaluation mode

true during F5/OpenCSG preview (and PNG export with --preview); false during F6 render and CLI mesh/2D exports. The standard knob for preview-speed vs render-quality tradeoffs.

Bound by the application before evaluation: GUI F5 -> true; GUI F6 -> false; CLI mesh/2D export (-o model.stl etc.) -> false; CLI PNG with --preview (or default PNG behavior) -> true; CLI PNG with --render -> false. Idioms: '$fn = $preview ? 24 : 120;', skipping minkowski()/expensive ops in preview, preview-only annotations.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Introduced 2019.05; on older versions reading it warns unknown-variable and yields undef — libraries feature-test with 'is_undef($preview) ? ... '.
  • CLI PNG default is preview mode, so $preview==true for plain '-o out.png'; --render flips it to false. VERIFY: '--preview=throwntogether' also true.
  • -D '$preview=true' injects a trailing top-level assignment that overrides the mode-provided value under last-write-wins — users do this to force preview-quality geometry in exports. VERIFY precedence via oracle.
  • An in-file top-level '$preview = false;' similarly overrides for the whole run. VERIFY.
  • It is an ordinary boolean binding, not magic: passing $preview=true into a subtree call re-binds it dynamically for that subtree only — legal and occasionally used.
  • Consistency requirement: %/# modifier visibility, color() visibility, and $preview must all agree with whichever mode your app claims to be in, or scripts render inconsistently.
$fn = $preview ? 24 : 96;
if ($preview) color("red") sphere(5); else sphere(5);
Implementer: Decide your app's preview story first, then set this coherently everywhere (including your PNG/thumbnail paths). It is just an outer-scope binding; only the override precedence needs pinning.

$t

$ var pre-2015.03 
$t = <number>  // default 0

Animation time, nominally in [0,1); driven by the GUI animation panel (FPS x Steps) or set externally; models read it to parameterize animated geometry.

valueCurrent animation fraction. Frame k of an n-step animation runs with $t = k/n.

Outside animation $t is 0. With the GUI animation panel active, the model is re-evaluated repeatedly with $t stepping through k/Steps; 'Dump Pictures' writes a PNG per frame. Ordinary dynamic-scoping rules apply: a script may assign $t itself (top-level assignment overrides the environment-provided value under last-write-wins), and may pass $t=x into calls.

Edge cases & oddities
  • $t never reaches 1.0 from the GUI stepper ($t = frame/steps, frames 0..steps-1); scripts written for looping animations rely on [0,1) semantics.
  • verify vs oracle a top-level '$t = 0.25;' in the script overrides the GUI/CLI-provided value for the whole run (last-write-wins over the injected outer value) — pin precedence via oracle.
  • CLI: --animate N is NEW in 2021.01 (pre-2021 releases required external -D '$t=...' looping); the frame number is appended to the -o output filename, 5-digit zero-padded.
  • -D '$t=0.5' works like any -D injection and wins over in-file assignment.
  • Reading $t when nothing set it yields 0, not undef — the default is a real binding in the outermost scope.
  • Geometry caching interacts: each distinct $t value forces re-evaluation of $t-dependent subtrees; $t-independent subtrees stay cached (performance surface, not semantics).
  • Non-numeric assignment is not special-cased: $t = "x" simply flows to whatever reads it, with normal type-mismatch warnings downstream.
  • The GUI animation panel and CLI --animate N drive $t through N frames of k/N (0..1), writing a frame%05d.png sequence; $t is 0 in ordinary (non-animated) runs. FPS/steps are GUI state, not language state.
$vpr = [60, 0, 360 * $t]; // spinning camera
rotate([0, 0, 360 * $t]) cube([10, 2, 2], center = true);
Implementer: Semantics are trivial (a pre-bound outer variable); the app decides how it is driven. Expose it in your MCP/CLI as an injectable binding. Only precedence vs in-file assignment needs an oracle probe.

$vpd

$ var 2015.03 
$vpd = <number>  // default 140

Viewport camera distance. Read = live camera; top-level assignment sets zoom after preview.

valueDistance from camera to the $vpt target.

Same model as $vpr/$vpt. The dist component of --camera populates it in CLI runs.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Default 140.
  • Zooming in the GUI changes the value read on the next recompile.
  • $vpd = 0 or negative: VERIFY — expected degenerate camera accepted or clamped by the viewer; the language variable itself stores any number.
  • With --projection=ortho the distance still controls the ortho scale (viewer behavior).
$vpd = 400; // zoom out
cube(50);
Implementer: Trivial; document viewer behavior for degenerate values rather than oracling them unless you promise GUI parity.

$vpf

$ var 2021.01 
$vpf = <number>  // default 22.5 (degrees)

Viewport camera field-of-view in degrees. NEW in 2021.01; same live-read/top-level-assign model as the other $vp* variables.

valueVertical field of view in degrees for the perspective camera.

Completes the camera quartet. Read returns the GUI camera FOV; top-level assignment applies after preview. Not present in 2019.05 and earlier.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Default 22.5 degrees.
  • On pre-2021.01 versions reading $vpf is an unknown variable (undef; VERIFY whether the unknown-$-variable warning fires) — feature-test material for scripts.
  • FOV <= 0 or >= 180: VERIFY clamping in the viewer.
  • Ignored under orthographic projection (viewer behavior), but the variable still reads/writes normally.
$vpf = 45;
sphere(10);
Implementer: Report it if you pin version() to 2021.01 — libraries may probe it. Trivial otherwise.

$vpr

$ var 2015.03 
$vpr = [x, y, z]  // default [55, 0, 25] (degrees)

Viewport/camera rotation in degrees. Reading returns the live GUI camera; top-level assignment moves the camera after each preview.

valueEuler rotation of the viewport in degrees, [x, y, z].

One of the viewport specials ($vpr/$vpt/$vpd/$vpf). In the GUI, reads reflect the camera state at evaluation time (so echo($vpr) tracks user orbiting after the recompile). A TOP-LEVEL assignment sets the camera once the preview compile finishes — combined with $t this scripts camera animation paths. In CLI runs the values reflect --camera arguments, or the defaults when none given.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Default [55, 0, 25] — the standard startup view; CLI exports without --camera evaluate with the defaults.
  • Assignments below top level do NOT move the camera; VERIFY: such an assignment still creates an ordinary dynamically-scoped variable readable in that subtree — pin via oracle.
  • The GUI writes the current camera into the variables BEFORE evaluation, so a script that both reads and assigns $vpr sees the pre-assignment (live) value in expressions evaluated before its own assignment position; last-write-wins ordering applies within the top scope. VERIFY exact read-vs-assign interleaving.
  • --camera=tx,ty,tz,rx,ry,rz,dist maps rx,ry,rz to $vpr; the alternate --camera=eyex,...,centerx,... form is converted to the rotational model. VERIFY converted values as read back.
  • Values are plain numbers in a 3-vector; nothing normalizes angles (e.g. 385 stays 385 when read back within the same evaluation; the GUI may normalize between frames — VERIFY).
  • Animation: assigning $vpr from $t is the documented camera-path idiom and must re-apply per frame.
  • In 2015.03..2019.05 era these existed with same defaults; $vpf joined in 2021.01.
$vpr = [70, 0, 360 * $t];
cube(10, center = true);
echo($vpr);
Implementer: For a headless/always-exact reimplementation: bind defaults, honor CLI camera injection, honor top-level assignment as 'camera request' output. The GUI live-read behavior only needs emulating if you build an interactive viewer; document your choice.

$vpt

$ var 2015.03 
$vpt = [x, y, z]  // default [0, 0, 0]

Viewport translation (camera look-at point). Read = live camera; top-level assignment repositions after preview.

valueCamera target translation vector.

Same read/assign model as $vpr: reads reflect current camera, top-level assignments take effect after the compile; CLI --camera's tx,ty,tz component populates it.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Default [0,0,0].
  • Non-top-level assignment does not affect the camera (ordinary $-variable otherwise).
  • GUI pan updates the value between evaluations; echo($vpt) after panning shows the new center on recompile.
  • --viewall/--autocenter CLI options recompute the camera; VERIFY what $vpt reads as in that case (the pre-adjustment value is expected, since adjustment happens after evaluation).
  • Assigning a wrong-shaped value (scalar, 2-vector): VERIFY behavior — expected: camera assignment ignored, possibly warning; the variable still holds the value for readers.
$vpt = [0, 0, 5];
translate([0, 0, 5]) sphere(2);
Implementer: Same as $vpr: treat as camera I/O channel; oracle-probe only if you claim GUI parity.

include vs use

include

directive pre-2015.03 (override-regardless-of-order rule: 2019.05) 
include <relative/or/absolute/path.scad>

Lexical inclusion of another .scad file: its top-level variables, module/function definitions, AND geometry all take effect in the including scope, as if pasted — with the quirk that main-file assignments override included ones.

pathAngle-bracket file path; no quotes, spaces allowed, everything up to '>' is the path; no escape mechanism, so '>' cannot appear in a filename.

Processed at parse time. The included file's statements execute in the including file's scope: geometry is instantiated, definitions are registered, and top-level assignments join the includer's top-level scope. Since 2019.05, assignments in the MAIN file override same-name assignments from included files REGARDLESS of textual order — this is the documented pattern for overriding library defaults (include <lib.scad> then 'width = 5;' wins even though lib assigned later-in-merged-order; and 'width = 5;' before the include also wins). Nested includes resolve their paths relative to the file containing the directive. No include guards exist: including a file twice re-executes it (duplicate geometry, duplicate-definition warnings).

Edge cases & oddities
  • Missing file: WARNING approximately "Can't open include file 'x.scad'." and evaluation continues with the file simply absent. VERIFY exact text.
  • Double inclusion duplicates geometry and re-registers definitions; duplicate definitions in one scope warn with last-one-wins. Observable warning surface.
  • Main-file-overrides-included is a 2019.05 behavior change; before that, plain last-write-wins textual order governed. Pin to the 2019.05+ rule for the 2021.01 baseline. VERIFY: whether includes nested two deep also lose to the main file (expected: any included assignment loses to a main-file assignment; between two included files, VERIFY which wins — expected later include wins).
  • include may appear inside a module body or block (it is lexical); the included content then lives in that inner scope. VERIFY: officially documented for use only as top-level-restricted; include's in-block legality and exact scoping must be pinned.
  • Recursive/self include: VERIFY whether 2021.01 detects the cycle (expected: it errors or hangs — pin the observed behavior and prefer a clean cycle error with matching text if any).
  • No semicolon after the directive; VERIFY whether a trailing ';' parses (as an error or tolerated).
  • Path search order on non-relative hits: including file's directory, then OPENSCADPATH entries, then the user library folder, then bundled installation libraries (see search-path entry).
  • -D and Customizer overrides beat included-file assignments (they inject after everything in the main scope).
  • Customizer does NOT surface parameters from included files (main file only).
  • The included file's geometry participates in the implicit union of the including scope at the position of the directive.
  • Windows path separators and case-sensitivity follow the host filesystem; write tests with forward slashes only for portability.
// lib.scad: r = 10; module ball() sphere(r);
include <lib.scad>
r = 3;   // overrides lib's r = 10 (2019.05+ rule)
ball();  // sphere of 3
Implementer: Implement as parse-time file splicing plus the special main-scope assignment precedence (tag assignments with their file-of-origin; main-file tags outrank). Oracle-probe: include-then-assign, assign-then-include, two includes assigning the same name, and double-include warning text.

use

directive pre-2015.03 (used-file variable evaluation: 2019.05) 
use <relative/or/absolute/path.scad>

Imports ONLY the module and function definitions from another file; the used file's top-level geometry is not instantiated and its top-level variables are private to it.

pathAngle-bracket file path, same lexical rules as include.

The used file is parsed and its top-level module/function definitions become callable in the using file. Its top-level geometry statements are NOT instantiated. Since 2019.05 its top-level variable assignments ARE evaluated — in the used file's own private scope — so its functions/modules see their own file's constants; those variables are invisible to and NOT overridable by the user (not by assignment, not by -D, not by the Customizer). Parametrize via arguments instead. use must appear at a file's top level.

Edge cases & oddities
  • use inside a block/module is a syntax error. VERIFY exact parser error text.
  • Pre-2019.05, used-file top-level variables were NOT evaluated (its functions saw undef) — material 2019.05 change; the 2021.01 baseline evaluates them.
  • Definitions from a use'd file are shadowed by same-name definitions in the using file (local wins). VERIFY precedence and whether a warning fires.
  • use is not transitive: modules that the used file itself use's are available inside the used file but are NOT re-exported to the user. VERIFY. (include inside a used file IS part of the used file's content and its definitions do carry through.)
  • Using the same file twice is effectively idempotent. VERIFY: no duplicate-definition warnings expected.
  • Missing file: WARNING approximately "Can't open library 'x.scad'." — VERIFY text (differs from the include message).
  • A file both include'd and use'd in one project behaves as both (double execution of definitions; geometry once via the include).
  • Customizer never shows used-file variables; -D cannot reach them (they are not in the main scope).
  • The used file's own $-variable reads still resolve dynamically at call time from the CALLER's dynamic environment (e.g. a used module honors the caller's $fn).
  • Search path identical to include.
// lib.scad: r=10; function rr()=r; module ball() sphere(rr()); ball();
use <lib.scad>
ball();      // sphere r=10; lib's top-level ball() call did NOT run
r = 3;       // does not affect lib's private r
Implementer: Model a used file as a separate top-level scope evaluated once (definitions + private variables, geometry discarded), exporting only its definition table. Keep dynamic $-scoping flowing through the boundary. Oracle-probe: shadowing precedence, transitivity, and both missing-file message texts.

import() and export formats

dxf_linear_extrude / dxf_rotate_extrude (deprecated)

module pre-2015.03 (deprecated; removed after 2021.01) 
dxf_linear_extrude(file, layer, height, ...); dxf_rotate_extrude(file, layer, ...)  // deprecated aliases, functional in 2021.01

Ancient DXF-extrusion modules — still functional in 2021.01 as deprecated aliases of linear_extrude/rotate_extrude with the legacy parameter order.

These are the same modules as linear_extrude/rotate_extrude reached through their legacy file=/layer= parameters — which is exactly why linear_extrude's true positional order starts (file, layer, height, origin, scale, ...). Calling them works in 2021.01 and emits the DEPRECATED-removal warning; the modern equivalent is an extrusion over a child import().

Edge cases & oddities
  • Emits 'DEPRECATED: The dxf_linear_extrude() module will be removed in future releases. Use linear_extrude() instead.' (and the rotate twin) — VERIFY exact wording per name.
  • All the classic deprecations — including child() — survived through 2021.01 as deprecated-but-functional builtins; none was removed by the 2021.01 baseline.
// deprecated, still works in 2021.01:
// dxf_linear_extrude(file="p.dxf", height=5);
// modern equivalent:
linear_extrude(5) import("p.dxf");
Implementer: Accept as aliases emitting the deprecation warning if strict 2021.01 parity is the goal; recognize-and-hint without executing is a documented divergence.

import

module pre-2015.03 (generic import; SVG+3MF read: 2019.05) 
import(file, convexity = 1, layer = <string, DXF only>, dpi = 96 <SVG only>)

Reads an external geometry file and instantiates it as a primitive: STL/OFF/AMF/3MF yield 3D solids; DXF/SVG yield 2D polygons. Format chosen by file extension.

filePath string, resolved relative to the invoking script's directory. Extension selects the parser (stl, off, amf, 3mf, dxf, svg).
convexityPreview-only ray-crossing hint for OpenCSG; never affects exported geometry. Default 1.
layerDXF only: name of the layer to import; omitted imports all layers. VERIFY: ignored silently for other formats.
dpiSVG only (2019.05+): dots-per-inch used to convert px units to mm; default 96 (1px = 25.4/96 mm).

Acts exactly like a built-in primitive at its position in the tree: transformable, boolean-composable, modifier-prefixable. Dimensionality is fixed by format (3D mesh formats vs 2D vector formats); mixing an imported 2D shape into a 3D boolean is the standard 'Mixing 2D and 3D objects' error. Meshes are imported as-is for preview; they must be manifold/closed to survive CGAL CSG or F6 render. Curved entities in DXF/SVG are tessellated at import time using the $fn/$fa/$fs in scope at the call.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Unknown/unsupported extension: ERROR approximately 'Unsupported file format while trying to import file' naming the path. VERIFY exact text.
  • Missing/unreadable file: WARNING and empty geometry; evaluation continues. VERIFY text (approximately "Can't open import file 'x'.").
  • verify vs oracle extension matching is case-insensitive ('.STL' works).
  • File path resolution: script directory; VERIFY whether library search-path tiers apply (see search-path entry).
  • convexity is parsed everywhere and ignored by exact pipelines — accept silently.
  • layer= on a non-DXF import: VERIFY silently ignored vs warning.
  • dpi < some minimum: VERIFY clamp/warning behavior.
  • Imported 3D meshes participate in preview un-validated; non-manifold input typically only errors at F6/CSG time (CGAL 'not closed' class errors) — deferred failure is observable behavior.
  • $fn/$fa/$fs at the call site affect DXF arc/circle and SVG curve tessellation. VERIFY exact application per entity.
  • Legacy import_stl()/import_dxf()/import_off() still FUNCTION in 2021.01 as deprecated aliases of import(), emitting 'DEPRECATED: The import_stl() module will be removed in future releases. Use import() instead.' (removal came only after 2021.01). dxf_dim()/dxf_cross() remain live separately.
  • Snapshot-only (2023+): OBJ import, center=true parameter, SVG id=/layer= element selection, 3MF color/material import — absent in 2021.01; flag as opt-in extensions only.
  • origin=[x,y] and scale= are honored for DXF import in 2021.01 (the legacy DXF pipeline); VERIFY: snapshot additions center= and id=/layer= selection for SVG import — pin exact versions before documenting as contract.
difference() {
  import("bracket.stl", convexity = 4);
  cylinder(h = 50, d = 5, $fn = 32);
}
Implementer: Route by extension into per-format readers (see companion entries). Keep failure modes non-fatal (warn + empty) exactly where OpenSCAD does; scripts rely on continuing. The deferred manifold-validation error timing (preview OK, render fails) must be emulated observably even in an always-exact engine — decide and document where you validate.

import_stl / import_dxf / import_off (deprecated)

module pre-2015.03 (deprecated 2011.12, alongside the introduction of unified import()) 
import_stl("file.stl"); import_dxf("file.dxf"); import_off("file.off");

Deprecated per-format aliases of import(), from before the unified importer.

filePath, resolved like import().

Each behaves as import() restricted to one format. In 2021.01 all three still function as deprecated aliases, emitting the DEPRECATED-removal warning on every use; they were removed only after 2021.01. Scripts in the wild (pre-2014 Thingiverse era) still contain them.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Functional in 2021.01: each call emits 'DEPRECATED: The import_stl() module will be removed in future releases. Use import() instead.' — VERIFY the exact per-name message text.
  • None of the classic deprecations (child(), assign(), import_stl/import_dxf/import_off, dxf_linear_extrude/dxf_rotate_extrude, triangles=) was removed by the 2021.01 baseline — all remain deprecated-but-functional; removals came only after 2021.01.
import_stl("part.stl"); // legacy — write import("part.stl");
Implementer: Implement as aliases of import() that emit the deprecation warning — 2021.01 parity requires them to work, and old scripts then run unmodified.

Customizer (comment-based parameters)

-D name=value injection (CLI overrides)

directive pre-2015.03 
openscad -D 'name=expr' [-D 'name2=expr2' ...] -o out file.scad

Ad-hoc command-line overrides injected as trailing top-level assignments in the main file's scope — they win under last-write-wins over everything textual.

nameAny variable name, including $-specials ($fn, $t, $preview...).
exprA full OpenSCAD expression, not just a literal; strings need embedded quotes: -D 'label="hi"'.

Each -D appends an assignment after the main file's own top-level statements, in command-line order. Because assignment is last-write-wins taking effect at the name's first-assignment slot, a -D value effectively replaces the in-file value everywhere. Reaches: main-file variables and (since include'd assignments share the main scope) included-file variables. Cannot reach: use'd files' private variables, or any non-top-level binding.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Multiple -D for the SAME name: later command-line occurrence wins (it is the later 'write').
  • verify vs oracle interaction order with -p/-P presets — expected: preset applied first, -D applied after, so -D beats the preset; pin.
  • expr is evaluated as an expression in the top scope: it may reference other top-level variables ('-D h=w*2'). VERIFY evaluation-order edge when it references a variable defined textually later (first-assignment-slot semantics make this subtle — pin).
  • Invalid expression syntax in -D: VERIFY error text and whether the run aborts.
  • -D 'x=' (empty): VERIFY.
  • Overriding a variable the file never defines: creates a new top-level variable (harmless; readable by the script if it references it — but a bare reference to it in the file would have warned before injection? No: injection happens before evaluation, so the name exists. VERIFY warning behavior).
  • -D can set $fn/$t/$preview/$vp* — standard automation idioms; must hit the same last-write-wins override path.
  • Shell quoting eats a layer: the canonical string form is -D 'name="value"'; document it.
  • -D applies only to the main file — a variable name that exists only in a use'd file is NOT affected (a new same-named main-scope variable appears instead, unused).
// shell: openscad -D 'wall=4' -D 'label="beta"' -o out.stl model.scad
wall = 2;
label = "prod";
echo(wall, label); // with the CLI above: ECHO: 4, "beta"
Implementer: Implement literally as appended assignments to the parsed main file before evaluation — that single mechanism reproduces every precedence quirk automatically. Pin the -D-vs-preset ordering and the forward-reference case with oracle probes.

Customizer group tabs: /* [Group] */, [Hidden], [Global]

syntax 2019.05 
/* [Group Name] */   |   /* [Hidden] */   |   /* [Global] */

Block comments containing a bracketed name partition subsequent parameters into named tabs; [Hidden] suppresses parameters from the panel; [Global] parameters appear in every tab.

A block comment whose entire content is '[Name]' starts a section: all following parameters (until the next section comment) belong to that tab. The special name 'Hidden' removes subsequent parameters from the panel entirely (they remain ordinary variables, still overridable via -D and presets — VERIFY preset applicability). The special name 'Global' makes its parameters visible in every tab. Parameters before any section comment go in an unnamed default section.

Edge cases & oddities
  • 'Hidden' and 'Global' are case-sensitive magic names. VERIFY case sensitivity.
  • verify vs oracle whether presets/-P apply values to Hidden parameters (expected: yes for -D; pin for preset sets saved by the GUI, which only round-trips visible widgets).
  • Reusing the same group name later in the file: VERIFY merge into one tab vs duplicate tab.
  • Tab order = order of first appearance in the source.
  • The section comment must be a standalone block comment '/* [Name] */'; VERIFY tolerance for extra whitespace and for '//'-style section comments (expected: block comments only).
  • Groups have no nesting.
  • A group with zero recognizable parameters: VERIFY whether an empty tab shows (expected: no).
  • Hidden is commonly used to keep derived-constant assignments from cluttering the panel even though computed RHS already disqualifies them — harmless overlap.
/* [Shell] */
wall = 2; // [1:5]
/* [Hidden] */
fudge = 0.01;
Implementer: Straightforward once the comment-aware scanner exists. Expose group membership in your MCP parameter schema; pin the two magic names' exact matching rules by GUI observation.

Customizer widget comment grammar

syntax 2019.05 
value; // [min:max] | [min:step:max] | [max] | [v1, v2, ...] | [v1:label1, v2:label2, ...] | <N>   (+ '// description' on the line above)

The trailing-comment mini-language that selects the widget for a parameter, and the preceding-line description comment.

[min:max]Slider from min to max. VERIFY step inference: 1 for integer bounds; pin decimal-bounds behavior.
[min:step:max]Slider with explicit step.
[max]Slider from 0 to max.
[a, b, c]Combo box (dropdown) of allowed values; numbers or unquoted strings.
[val:Label, ...]Combo box with display labels; stored value is the part before ':'.
<N> (bare number comment, strings)VERIFY: for string parameters, '// 12' sets the text box maximum input length.
// description (line above)A '//' comment on the line immediately above the assignment becomes the parameter's tooltip/description text.

The comment must trail the assignment on the same line (after the ';'). Widget by type+comment: number+range -> slider; number no comment -> spinbox; number+list -> dropdown; string+list -> dropdown; string no comment -> textbox; boolean -> checkbox (comment ignored); vector (<=4 numeric elements) -> one spinner/slider per component, with a single range comment applying to every component.

Edge cases & oddities
  • Labeled values: '// [10:Small, 20:Large]' — labels may contain spaces; VERIFY whether labels may contain ':' or ',' (expected: no escaping mechanism).
  • String dropdowns use UNQUOTED values in the comment ('// [red, green, blue]'); the assignment RHS is a quoted string and must match one entry to preselect.
  • verify vs oracle '// [max]' single-value slider form (0..max) exists in OpenSCAD proper (Thingiverse-inherited grammar) — pin.
  • Malformed range comments (e.g. '// [a:b]' with non-numbers on a number param): VERIFY fallback (expected: treated as plain spinbox / dropdown attempt; no console warning).
  • Whitespace tolerance inside brackets: '// [ 0 : 5 ]' — VERIFY accepted.
  • A range comment on a boolean or a list comment on a bool: comment ignored, checkbox stands. VERIFY.
  • Vector + range: '// [0:50]' bounds every component; per-component ranges do not exist.
  • Description comment must be IMMEDIATELY above (no blank line); VERIFY blank-line tolerance and multi-line ('//' stacked) behavior.
  • verify vs oracle step in '[min:step:max]' with step > span, zero step, min > max — pin GUI behavior (expected: unusable/clamped silently; no console warnings — the Customizer never warns).
  • The grammar lives in comments: /* */ block comments trailing the line are NOT widget comments (only '//'). VERIFY.
  • Unicode in labels/descriptions passes through (UTF-8).
// Wall thickness of the box
wall = 2; // [0.5:0.1:5]
finish = "matte"; // [matte:Matte, gloss:High gloss]
size = [20, 30]; // [10:100]
Implementer: Write the comment grammar as a real grammar with tests, not regex soup — Thingiverse/Printables models exercise every corner. All VERIFY items are cheap GUI-observation probes against 2021.01; none affect evaluation, only the panel and your MCP schema.
